McDOUGALUS Patelit SELF-RAISING FLOUR ONCE TRIED The Examiner, Saturday, December 29, 1906

[ALL RIGHTS WESETtVED.I

"I'm afraid _I -0:13 prrocouplfil," Ate *aid,

srailing very pleasantly, "you must feriive me. 'When a man comes ht.aile after a loup; absence and finds himself among dear old friends, on such an OCCllllloll—aud, I hope, dear-to-be new ones, too—he is to be forgiven, is .he not, i. he is .a little thoughtful ? Oh, we mt.nage- t..ings in Australia in much the same way among the upper classes; the people there are mostly English." "Many, I suppose, though, left their country for their country's good ? " she said, with a smile. "Didn't we send over convicts there once to New South Wales, or Botany Bay, or some- .

_ His glance b;?caree very direct for an instant. Had the woman a suspicion ? No, no, it was a

chance word.

"I believe so," he answered, quietly. "Now PO keep them nearer home, many just here, at Dartmoor. I hear one poor beggar escaped last night; I wonder have they caught him yet? If not, I should think they will not. ",And I supp9so you hope they will not ? " . . . .

The hum oiii Of tl;e question sfruch him. "Oh, .most decidedly, I hope they will not," he answered. "Don't you ? " • She looked dubious for a minute. "I really don't know that I care very much one way or the other,"- she nswered. "I suppose one generally has what might -almost be called a perverted sympathy with a man fighting for his liberty. For all that, he would not have been a prisoner in Dartmoor had he been a person to be esteemed. For the sake of society at large, it would be better, probably, that he should be caught. They are going into breakfast now, shall we go?" Vernon Salisbury smiled to himself as he made hie way to the handsome room where lark altrast was to be served. Yesterday at this time he had been hard at work, habited in the dress of a eonviet, with Armed warders standing over him; to-day he was a gentleman at large, wising in good society, already the object of

more than ono% woman's admiration! The situation VNa pertainly humororm Of all thT company he wa.l the handsomest man, the most healthy-looking; his skin was fresh as. that of a young girl, his eyes as clear. Even Olive Brandon looked at him once or twice, admiring him in an impersonal way. More than a little jealous, Paul Clifford whispered to her: "la that fellow going to stay, Olive? I shan't half like it if he does.

"Why not? What does it matter? Surely, you would not be jealous?" •

yes." "How shocking! Don't you know jealousy is a sin?"

I "It's natural—the man's too horribly good- ; looking • and, of course, he has eyes to see how ; beautiful you arc." I "You don't trust me, then ? You don't belieye what I told you in the rose garden ?" I. He managed for a minute to clasp her bands in his; no one was looking at them—at all events, no one but Vernon Salisbury. Everyone else had eyes only for the bride and bridegroom.

"I do trust you," he answered. "I know quite well you'll be as true to me always as I shall be to you; but it is reasonable, is it not, that I should begrudge this man the honour of your company, which he will have if he stays? And I don't see how he'll he able to help falling in love with you. I believe he's half in love now." "Nonsense! "she said. "He's perfectly satisfied with Mrs. Cole. How handsome she looks. I never saw her look better. I thought to-day would have been a blow to her; it seems I was mititaken."

' P au ifford glanced at the widow; she was i talking with much animation to Vet non Salisbury, making him laugh now and then. For all that, she did not really engage his attention; 1 his thoughts were with Olive Brandon; his eyes mostly turned in her direction. Now and then he glanced at the bridegroom. John Brandon I seemed to have forgotten his crime of the night before--the present cmb.trrassment of the situa- tion. He had won the one woman he wanted; she was now his wife, his to have and to hold until death them did part—nothing else seemed I to matter.

His face was perfectly radiant; his eyes dwelt with adoring love on the pale face of his bride. She was not looking her best, because she was not marrying a man she loved, but to him her face was the most beautiful in the whole wide world.

She was not unhappy; she was not sorry the final step had been taken, but her feelings differed very much from his. His near presence was not distasteful, but it was not a delight; her eyes when they met his were cold, a little hard. Vernon Salisbury bent near to Mrs. Cole. "If ever I marry," ho said—and she felt her heart leap—"l hope my bride will give me prettier, more loving glances than Mrs. Brandon gives dear old Jack. 6.11 the love is upon one side, is it not 9"

"Most of it, any way," Mrs. Cole returned. "I think she likes him, has a lukewarm sort of affection for him; but it was the brother Whom she really eared for. He died in South Africa. Did you know him ? "

Vernon Salisbury thought of the night before;. of the horror hidden in the safe, which he had carried out into the darkness of night. He was ty- man almost wthout nerves, and yet he shuddered. know him ?" he repeated. "I wonder do we evr know anyone? I have met him, of course. I should fancy a woman has a better chance of happiness with Jack." Mrs. Cole shook her head. "I don't know," she answered. "We women are quite as fond of giving love as receiving it. Celia will never, I think, be able to give hers to John Brandon; it's buried in Dick's grave. Ah! we are to be victimised• with a speech—one escapes that at most up-to-date weddings." But the speeches were not long. The happy pair were making a long journey, and had to start fairly early. When the bride went to change her dress, John Brandon took Vernon Salisbury to the study. "You want money, of course? " he said. "How much ? "

Vernon Salisbury flung himself into a chair, and smiled at John Brandon. "Oh nothing to speak of," be answered. "A

few pound notes will do me. I shall have next door to no expenses. If you give me the address of your tailor I can order sonic clothes, and you can settle later on. Don't bother about me in any way. I shall be all right until you come back." John 'Brandon's face grew troubled. "All right? Where?" he asked. "Why, at your place, to be sure, dear old chap—close to my old quarters, in the charming society of your adorable sister and Mrs. Cole. Don't say anything against the plan, because I've made up my mind to stay—and I shall stay." John took a turn up and down the room. What could he do? Nothing. This man had the whip hand of him; and yet to leave him in the same house as his sister--a convict—it was horrible! "It's not safe here," he said. "Warder Sullivan met me as we returned to the house; he says anyone else but Chief Warder Clark would have recognised you; that your eyes give you away. He told me to tell you to get away." Vernon Salisbury nodded. "I daresay, but I shall stay." "Why?" "In the first place, because I know I shall be comfortable, because I feel :are; in the second place, because I'm half in love with, your beautiful sister." John Brandon turned on his companion in a rage, his hand lifted; he felt as though some noxious reptile had stung him—then his.arm dropped. "Don't dare to say anything like that again! he said, "or--or no consideration for my own safety will prevent me giving you up, and taking the consequences. I- tell you, you can't stay here!" "And I tell you I will! As for your sister, are you so blind that you cannot see I am likely to get no return for the admiration I must give her? Didn't you recognise the young people were next door to, if not quite, plighted lovers, when we saw them in the garden ? I should like to cut him out; if I thought it possible I could succeed I would try, but I recognise it is not possible. Go away without any misgivings. Unless I am very much mistaken, Mrs. Cole will see Miss Brandon does not have much of my company—the good lady is more than half in love with me already. That's the worst of widows, they will take the initiative—they won't wait for the mall to do the wooing." Jaok did not answer at once, he quite recog- nised that he was powerless in this matter. If Vernon Salisbury made up his mind to stay, he could not prevent him; seemingly, too, hi could . not frighten him. He either believed he was safest- here. or he was utterly reckless of souse-

quences. For as instant he wondered whether he would be wise to turn traitor with regard to this man —to inform the prison authorities anonymously that he was there, to risk the chance that he also would turn traitor. After all, it would do the convict no good to get him into trouble.

"You don't consider," he said, "how awkward it will be for me if you .arc caught hero."

Vernon Salisbury' lifted his-handsome eye- brows, and opened his beautiful eyes very wide. "But, my dear chap, I do," he answered—"l realise that more than anything else; it will mean a hanging job, for your safety and mine are now bound up. So long as lam free you are free—no longer! Do you think it's wise to talk to me so long ? To me it seems a little risky. I fancy we'd better say good-bye. Give me sonic loose cash. I'll let you know when I want more."

John Brandon gave up the job in despair. The man meant to stay—would stay—would mac himself quite at home in his house; get on friendly terms with his sister. It was all too horrible, and he did not even know what tho man's crime had been. Some offence against the law, not against life, Warder Sullivan had saiJ —there-was just a grain of comfort in that. He pushed some money towards him, then without another word he went from the room.

Everyone was waiting in the hall, the bride, dressed for travelling, was coming downstairs. Quarter of an hour later adieux had been said, and bride and bridegroom had driven off. Vernon Salisbury trolled up to Olive Brandon, who was sitting in the comparative seclusion of a bay window with her lover. Mrs. Cole, the lady who, as far as possible. acted the part of mother towards (-Aire Brandon. was a good-looking woman on the wrong side oi fifty, though she certainly did not lock it. ile: one wish in life since her widowhood had hen to settle herself again. Marriage has its draw- baek=, no d'oebt— her own had not been particu- larly happy—but it has one advantage, on recommenontion as a rule it constitutes a provision for one's old age. Marring° is, generally, aS good, at all events, as an anruity, and, for a woman, much eheaper to obtain. Mrs. Cole had ',ad deeigns upon John Brandon. Ti Dick had hot gone away, and, at everyone supposed, died in a dietant country, there was, of course, some likrliticed that in the end he Might have married her. Life for a man, lived without the company of a woman, becomes, after a time, as a rule, veryelonely. If Celia Verde had been beyond Johnill reach, when Olirs married, ten chances to one he weuld have asked Mrs. Cole to share his name. She was older than he; he had cared for -her only as a friend— scarcely as that. The thing would, however, most likely have come to pass. Mrs. Cole bad no; gone to the church. Lady Caroline, who naturidly wanted to be present at the marriage, had ast.ed her to receive the,. bride and bridegroom and the rest of the guests on their return from tire Ceremony. She kissed Celia as she came into, the house.; she had ',ea jealous of the girt, bat she was wise enough to realiso that jealousy was now more than foolish. John Brandon was beyond her reach; she was sorry, but still was not so sorry to-day as she had been yestereay, because at brenkfast that morning she had seen a man whom she felt she could very much prefer even to John Brandon. He was quite young, he had just returned from abroad; he had mentioned, trite casually, as though it were an ordinary remark, that he„liad not seen a beautiful woman until that morning for he did not know how long. Ile had locked at her, she was sure, with admiration, and when John Brandon went on his heneymoon, he was to atop and pay her and Olive a visit. He had a charming name—Vernon Salisbury— she thought she had never heard a more attrac- tive one, and it suited him so perfectly. He was handsome— so handsome that at breakfast John Brandon had looked a little ordinary; she had wondered how she could over have admired him. He had a bright, breezy manner, and although he talked nonsense, it really seemed preferable to sense. She had taken him round the garden; he had plucked her a little flower—she was Wearing it now. With something approaching a blush, she told herself a woman was a fool who could not marry a man with whom she was thrown into daily, hourly contact. She quite forgot that 811 c had failed to marry John Brandon; that she had don. her best and ailed. Defeat had not only not aisheaitened her, elle bad elven forgotten she had been defeated. She was certainly looking very handsome. Many man, no doubt, would have admired her more than they would a young girl. Personally, she was like those many men, she admired hereelf very much. "I wish I could have been earlier with nay congratulations," she said to Celia, "but one eisn't,unfortunately, be in two places at the same time. Who is that your husband is talking to?" The girl started, then she flushed, shyly, then she frowned. "Oh, for goodness sake, don't call him that!" she said, "it sounds so old- fashioned. It's one of the warders from Dartmoor. It seems a prisoner escaped last night, they thought he had taken refuge in Jack's house, and searched it. I suppose the man has come to tell us they have caught him—they always do catch them, do they not ?" Mrs. Cole nodded. "Generally, I believe," she answered. "But how very inconsiderate of the man to come worrying here on a wedding- day. Just as though anyone cares whether the wretch is caught or not—so long, of course, as he'does not come near one. Are you going stairs ? Shall I tell John when he conies in? He's sure to ask first thing where you are." Celia shrugged her shoulders. 'Just as you like," she said. "I do hope Jack is not going to be one of those husbands who never leave one alone. I shall hate being married more even than I expect to be, if be is!" For some reason or other Mrs. Cole smiled. Perhaps she was glad it did not seem very likely John Brandon would hare a very rosy time with his young wife. "A foolish hope," she said. "There are only two kinds of husbands, my dear, the ones who love us almost too much, and the ones who do not love us enough. Believe me, it is better to 114 re tno !TIIIPh love than too little." "Celia paused. Hi, there 7 then, no happy medium ? ' she asked. "No," Mre, Cole returned. "What now items to you it happy medium, in a week's time you would look upon as neglect." The fault had been the bride's, no doubt. "Mrs. Brandon has gone upstairs," she answered, "she won't be a minute. Sho's probably gone to take off her veil; most brides do that when they come home. HEARTY AT EIGHTY.

lbw Bile B.aans 'Buislied al Old Man's Body Pains and Sleeplessness.

Although iu his eightieth year, Mr.o wen Mul-lowney, furniture remover, of 15; Church-road, Seacombe. Cheshire, is in the enjoyment of all his faculities and is perfectly happy in what he will not consent to look upon as his declining days. This excellent state of health he attributes to Bile Beans. Interviewed by a local ne.vspaper reporter Nlr. Nlaldowney said : Up to twelve inontha ago I hal never been ill. Then I had pains in the bo_ly,,and I could neither eat nor drink. The very sight of food upset me, and I felt very miserable. I conld do no more than just dose a bit at- nights. I used to wake up out of these snatches of sleep and shout out at the top of my voice. Then I saw funny visions, and I fancied I was in for/%r very bad illness. I was attended by a doctor, but got no better. Then I read about a similar case to mine being cured by Bile Beans; and lat once gave them a trial. I regained my appetite, and was soon able to 'sleep peacefully as. before, and get up in the morning ready for my work. Now I am in grand health, and I have no hesitation in. saying that it is due to Bile Beaus alone."

"Sure that he is dead ? I don't want him dead. I love him. But I must know—l must ! " . He drew her head even closer; he stooped and kissed her trembling lips. "I am perfectly sure," he returned. It is a jumble of the years' events, crazes, songs, and slang, and if it is not very instructive It is at least more amustog than many of the annual reviews, which many of us are only too glad to get rid of the old year to stop to read. A Talented Amateur Actress —lt does not appear to be generally known, that Mrs. William James of West Dean Park, who by the way has won—and lately added to—considerable fame in amateur theatricals, is a daughter of one of the lovely Moncrieffe sisters, whose beauty made such a sensation in London in the sixties. One, it will be remembered, became Duchess of Athole, another Countess of Dudley, yet another Lady Forbes of Newo, Mrs. James's mother, while another was the unfortunate Lady Mordaunt, West Dean Park, Chichester, is a Jacobean house, possessing a wonderful oak pannelled hall. It is a famous piece for week end • gatherings during the summer and of large shooting parties at this season of the year. His Majesty the King bas,upon several occasion honoured Mr.and James with a visit to West Dean Park. The Custom of Giving Presents.—Christmas never passes without ourhearing something about the nuisance of presents. We receive gifts from our friends that we do not much want, and have to spend money to returra presents, which may be of little use to them. Much may be done by the exercise of a little care and fore- thought to obviate such disadvantages, but from a utilitarian point of view' there is some truth in the old saying that presents are the dearest things one can buy. They are all very well for children, or when given by those with a super-abundance, to those less endowed with this worlds goods, but in ordinary circumstances, there is, no doubt, a good deal of money wasted by the custom of giying presents. In France this custom is, if anything, more univeraal than in this country, but the French make the New Year, and not Christmas, the occasion for distributing their gifts to one another. Winter Sleep.—Not until January is well advanced do we begin to notice any perceptible change in the earlier coming of day dawn, but we are told upon . medical authority that the long winter nights have their compensations, in that they are conducive to '. sound sleep. In the summer time the sun's rays stimu- late both mental and bodily energies, before we can secure a full night's rest, although one would think that much depends upon how effectually we darken our bedroom. It is certain that the sleep of some people is much more easily disturbed by day-light than is the case with others, and although we may have the benefit of a long night's rest, most of us are more reluctant to leave our beds in dark winter mornings than in tho summer time. Few like the cold and cheerless morn- ings,and we shall all be glad enouglawhen the lengthen- ing days once more bring the promise of spring. 'raking Cold.—Tt is suggested.that if we made up our minds not to take coldave should escape such inflictions which .re so prevalent at this time of year. Dr. Road, in referring to this subject says " we should try what a vigorous action of will power will do, for a strong resolve not to take cold, lam persuaded, can ward off much harm." There is more than a popular suspicion that doctors know really very little about colds, but this is en experiment which at least we can all try. Whether we are to tea all the time, or only at the first symptoms of a cold, does not appear, but an any case a strong 'eterinination that we have not a cold, even if we have really got one, will help us over the trouble, if it does not induce us to take undue risks. In any ordinary circumstances, it is half the battle to make as little as possible of small ailments, and that is probably the point Dr. Read has chiefly in view. Jewels and Gowns.—Women who possess beautiful jewels, show a disposition to dress up to them. For, example, owners of emeralds select shades of green, those who possess an array of rubies choose red hues, while the women who have many turquoise ornaments are dressing in blue. The case is the same in the matter of semi-precious stones, and with amethysts in such high favour, mauve is very popular. There is much to be said in praise of this notion,and the woman who was heard to remark that the most successful toilette she ever possessed was built up to a row of blue beads, is doubtless only one of many who have had a similar happy experience. Evening footwear.—Silver and gold shoes are leading with festive evening attire, but there are rumours to the effect that ere the winter is past, the white steers- ing and black leather shoes, beloved by our ances- tresses, will be seen again. Indeed one smart dresser, very well known in Society, has been observed, on several•occasions. to be wearing the old world white stockings, and black shoes finished with bright red rosettes. Artificial Flowers.—When " corsage knots," or floral ornaments for the hair, are in requisition, artificial blooms and foliage are nowadays invariably preferred, and to such perfection has the art of flower-making • arrived, that detection is almost impossible, %Of course the best flowers are very expensive, but with reasonable care they can be made to last a long time. The fashion- able women provides herself with a goodly selection of sprays and " knots " which, by the way are all cor- rectly scented, and then rings the changes as the seasons .""-• come round or Dame Fashion dictates. Spanish Lace Fashionable.—There is quite a craze for the black Spanish lsce robe, which to be a - success must be mounted on black chiffon, or crepe de , chene, and worn over a soft black glace slip. Many aS modistes are ornamenting the hem of the skirt with glittering black bugles, or pailettes, and undoubtedly the notion makes -for smartness. Moreover it affords weight, and therefore insures the robe falling grace- .! fully. At the Jannery sties many bargains in lace tobea promise to be offered, and those among us who ,can afford such a luxury should not fail to secure it. Rabbits and Ptomaine Poisoning.—A curious theory of ptomaine poisoning was suggested the other day at an inquest on a woman who had died from this cause after eating boiled rabbit. The doctor said that be had known such cases before, and that the danger lay in the rabbit having been chased and eaten shortly after it was killed. The terror and exhausion of the anirnal,he suggested, engendered the poison, but if this is so, one Would suppose that the same results would be pro- duced in the hare, which is commonly eaten after being killed at caursing contests. It is not altlgetiser satis- factory that so little appears to be be known about the origin of ptomaine poison and cases of it have occurred in persons who had partaken of what, so far as investigation could show, was perfectly sound and healthy mast.

SAD BURNING FATALITY.

Warrington Stationmaster's

Bereavement.

The Christmas season was locally marked by an exceptionally sad burning fatality. On Sunday night Mrs: Ellen Gordon, 25, Thynne-street, wife of Mr. John Gordon, night stationmaster at Bank Quay, was in her kitchen with a lighted taper in her hand when she fainted anal fell to the floor. Her clothing became ignited and ahe was severely burned before her daughter, who was alarmed by her screams, procured assistance. Mr. S. Brighouse conducted the inquest on Monday at the Police Station. John Gordon said that on Sunday night he was on duty at Bank Quay Station when he heard that his wife had been burnt. He at once went home and found Dr. Fox had been called in, and had ordered her removal to the Infirmary, to which place he (witness) conveyed her. Previous to the removal he asked his wife how the aczident happened, and she replied, " I was going from one kitchen to another with a lighted taper in my band, when I had one of my ' fainty bouts ' and fell, and I remember no more." His wife had been for some time subject to attacks of faintness. He afterwards asked his daughter Martha, aged 18, how the accident happened, and her reply was: "1 was in bed when I beard mother scream. I came downstairs and found her with her clothing on fire. I rushed out aud saw a pcaktman, whom I brought in, and be put out the flames.' The Coroner said he understood the deceased lady had, prior to the accident, been getting ready for the Christmas festivities.

The jury verdict of" Accidental death." The Foreman, Mr. F. J.Whittle, said the jury wished t? express, through the Coroner, their sympathy with Mr. Gordon in the sad loss he had sustained. They did so not only as jurymen, but as townspeople who had to come into contact with Mr. Gordon officially all the year round. He was sure the inhabitants of the borough generally would also sympathize with him.

SCIENCE NOTES AND NEWS,

:VELOCITY IN GUNNERY.

QC eery interesting problem in gunntay been raised by Brigadier-General Crozier, Chief of Ordnance in the United States Army, in his annual report. He holds that the 12-inch gun now in use in most of the coast fortifications of the United States would not last through as engagement of two hours—the time that would elapse from the time the leading vessel of • fleet would come within the range until the last vessel would pass beyond the range of the guns. General Crozier thinks it is of the utmost irn• portance that some method be devised by which the necessary gun power can be obtained with less expense that that involved in using the very high velocities of projectiles now employed, with the accompanying rapid wearing away of tips rifling in such a manner as to destroy the accuracy of the gun after a few rounds.

VESUVIUS DUST IN PARIS.

On April sth, 1906, a somewhat remarkable Mg occurred in Paris. It was a dry, yellowish fog, and was sufficiently dense to interfere with navigation on the Seine. To investigate the nature of this fog M. Stanislsus Meunier, the well- known Parisian meteorologist* exposed plates covered with glycerine on the roof. The dust thus collected was treated with thoulet, liquid for separating minerals, a solution of potassium- mercuric iodide of specific gravity rather greater than 3. The heavier particles of the dust which sank in this liquid were found to consist

of a fine sand; and on comparing this with a sample of Veeuvian ash which fell in 1822 M. Meunier found them identical, except that the Paris duet contained a number of perfectly spherical globules of oxide of iron which were wanting in the other. Thus it appeara that tla• fog in Paris was caused by the dust of a recent eruption of Vesuvius floating in the air.

===== 0002601_19061229_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
EARTH IS STILL YOUNG.

It has been stated by such authorities as Eel- yin, Newcomb, and Ball that the future of the sun's activity will be comparatively short—not more than 10,000.000 years—and some have even suggested that the sun's activity already shews signs of waning. So far is this from being the ease, says a writer in the 4117 antic Monthly, that only one-fourth of our supply of energy has been expended, and three-fourths are yet ist store for tho future life of the planetary system. This opens up to our contemplation a decidedly re- freshing view of the future, and will give re- newed hope to all who believe that the end of mundane progress is not yet in sight. Not only should the future possibilities of scientific pro- gress be vastly extended, but there will in all probability be the most ample time for the fur- ther development of the races of beings inhabit- ing this planet. According to this view, the evolution of our earth is still in its infancy, with the zenith of its splendour far in the future.

===== 0002601_19061229_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
X-RAYS AS HAIR DARKENERS.

Not the least curious of the effects produced by the X-rays are those noticed by Dna. Imbert and Marques. The former is a professor in the medical faculty at Montpellier, and the latter is his assistant, and while these gentlemen were experimenting with the X-rays it was observed that the hair and beard of one of them, which bad been almost white, were restored to their natural colour. They were, in fact, observed to have become even darker than they were origin- ally. Again, in the case of a man whom these same experimenters werd treating with the X- rays for lupus, the hair first became grey and then turned dark again. And it is found to have remained dark, although it has not been treated with the rays for some months, and has been fre- quently cut. Other observations of the 8111320 experimenters confirm the fact that the X-raye turn light hair dark. It now remains to be see* whether the effect is permanent, and whether the treatment can be applied without risk.

COUNTING THE STARS. Stars visible to the unaided eye are numberei •by thousands, those to be seen through tele- scopes are estimated in millions, and it has been usually supposed that 100,000,000 is the number of all the stars visible through the great tele- scopes. According to a calculation made by Mr. Ellard Gore and published in the ONerratory, this is, however, probably an over-estimate. Tie obtain his results, Mr. Gore counted the num- bers of the stars in a number of the photographs made by the late Dr. Roberts of stellar regions, - distinguishing them as photographs made in the neighbourhood of the Milky Way and-. those made in regions that lie without it. Combining these results with Professor Pickering's estimate of the relative areas of Milky Way stars and of other star spaces, he obtains the --rand total for all the stars of 64,184,757. This is possibly an under-estimate, because some of the fainter stars might not appear in these photographs, compre- hensive as they are.

CAUSES OF HAY FEVER.

What. is the cause of that somewhat mysterious disease 'known as hay fever? Some interesting facts concerning it have been given in MMus- tration. During the summer the air is often charged with the pollen of wind-fertilised plants. and it is the inhaling of this pollen which is said to produce the symptoms of hay fever. The pol- len of. different species of plants is said to pos- sess different degrees of activity, and that of some is innocuous. The fact that so many dis- eases have been traced to a bacterial origin will suggest to some that bacteria are conveyed along with the pollen. and may be the real cause of 'hay fever. But Dunbar claims to have proved that the pollen itself, and not bacteria, is the cause. Some 114 plants are known to have the toxic principle in their pollen. Among these are mheat, rye, and a number of grasses. This lat- ter fact is some justification for the term hay fever. The mischief is said to be caused by a granular arnylaceous material in the pollen. From the poisonous principles of a number of pollens antitca;os have been nrenareil and

GREAT FRENCH CHEMIST.

M. Henri MOissan (of Paris) the . winner of the £7,500 Nobel prize for chemistry in 1906, is well known as the producer of synthetic dia. monds and rubies by means of the electrio furnace. His earliest work of importance was the isolation of the elsment fluorine. The pro- Ituction of this gas had defied cl4mists for the previous eighty years, the rea n being that fluorine is of such a remarkably energetic nature that it attacks and combines with glass and all metals of which chemical vessels are usually made. Moissan isolated fluorine by working with a vessel made of an alloy of platinum and trklium. The invention of the electric furnace was his most important work. By the device of confin i - ing an electric arc-flame inside a narrow vessel formed of 'lime and carbon he was able to pro- Noe a temperature ,of 3,500 deg. to 4,ooodeg: Centigrade. The temperature of ' the limelight flame, previously the hottest known, is only f,ooodeg. At these high temperatures he has been able to prepare a whole world of chemical substances which are of great scientific interest. Incidentally he has commercialised the produc- tion of acetylene gas—calcium carbide is made in the electric furnace—and manufactured artificial diamonds.

===== 0002601_19061229_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
COLOURS AND TIIE SENSES.

The relation of colour to sensation is discussed by a German scientist, Herr Gieseler, in a Leipsic publication. That light has a decided effect on the sense organs is well known in a general way, as is the effect of colours to a lees extent, but Herr Giessler examines the experiments in detail. These showed that the sense of hearing was dis- turbed by red, yellow, green, and blue, not only the subjective sensations being affected but also the location of the source of the sound. ' The sense of equilibrium is affected by colours, al- though the most remarkable effects are produced lat the sense of taste, where in the majority of oases we find an increase or a decrease of the sensation power. Sometimes the taste of some- thing sweet is changed, upon seeing certain colours to salty or bitter. In many cases ft was found that the colours which produce the most intense effects in the various individual were stated by the subjects to be their favourite cc:fours. In the case of temperature, blue and violet cause a decrease, red and green an in- crease in the sensation of cold; covering the eyes causes a weakening of the sense of hearing, while strong light instantly increases the acute- ness of the ear. Red, yellow, and green raise the pitch of a deep tone, while violet causes a high tone to deepen, and even the sense of taste may be affected by the amount or light present without regard to colour.

Herr Giessler says that another experimenter, Giesbach, found that the sense of touch in per. ions born blind was below normal instead of be- ing above, as is commonly supposed. It is alio found that the other senses suffer from the lack of the sense of sight. For example, smokers are frequently unable in the dark by means at trio sense or amen to tell wnetner or not a cigar is lit, while in darkness the sharpest sense ot taste is not able to distinguish between Atli* which have really different tastes.

An unknown man should have appeared before the Larnbeth Court on Wednesday to answer the charge of ,being drunk and incapable at Rye-lane, Peckham, on Christmas Eve.—Assistant-Gaoler Neal informed the magistrate, however, that after his arrest the man was removed to the Camberwell Infirmary, where he died on Christmas afternoon. Orders have been given for special efforts to be made in the Kent end Sussex Regimental Districts to enlist tailors for the fitla Lancers, now stationed in the Transvaal. A reinforcement for the regiment, from the 21st. Lancers, is to embark in the Braemar Castle about the 10th of January.

In a burglary at Dover on Christmas night jewellery value £23 was stolen. The magistrates on Wednesday cbmmitted them to Quarter Sessions.

1 1:'•::IWIEW OF THE YEAR.

NOTABLE EVENTS IN 1906.

The year 1006 will be memorable, at home from• the political point of view for the re- markable wave of Liberalism which swept through the land, and 'after being in Opposi- tio.n for eleven years,. a Liberal Government was returned to power by a tremendous majority. Abroad the year has been remark- able for the large number of catastrophes cf appalling magnitude which have visited various parts of the world, among. these be- ing the terrible mining disaster m France, the eruption of Vesuvius, and the destruc- tion wrought by earthquake in both North and South America. _ _ . . .

In the social' world the most important event was the marriage of King Alfonso and Princess Ena of Battenberg, while our own King and Queen were as busy among the people as ever, His Majesty performing ninny public functions, though Queen Alexandra suffered a sad loss in the death of her. father, King Christian of Denmark. Happily tte year has passed without war between any of th.i great Powers, but Russia has suffered much from political dissensions, while in South Africa there was a good deal of fight- ing owing to trouble with a 'Natal • chief, Bambaata. •

PARLIAMENTARY AND POLITIC AL.

The General Election, which occurred early in January, resulted in the return of 430 Liberal and Labour candidates and 83 Nationalists, a total of 513, against 157 Unionists. This gave the Ministerial party a majority of 356, which is the largest sinbe the passing of the Reform Bill in 1832, when the Liberals were returned With a total of 370 above their opponents. One of the most remarkable features of the election was the success of the Labour Party, no fewer than 52 being elected out of 90 who went to the poll. Altogether .the Liberal_ and Labour parties gained 228 seats, while the Unionists succeeded in winning 16, of which eight had been captured from them at contests since the general election of 1900. The only member of the Government to be defeated' was Captain Freeman Thomas, who had been appointed a Junior Lord of the Treasury,. and who was beaten at HaStings by 413 votes. Several. vacancies have been caused by deaths and .resignations, but—the only seat captured by the Conservatives was Cocker- mouth, where Sir J. S. Bandies regained the seat which Sir Wilfrid Lawson had won at the General Election. Mr. Balfour, who was defeated at Manchester, found a seat in tile City of London (where the Hon. A. Gibbs re- tired), and beat Mr. Gibson Bowles, who lost his seat at King's Lynn and stood for the City as a Free Trader, by 11,340 votes. Later, on Sir Edward Clarke resigning his seat (owing to difference of opinion with his con- stituents on the (juestion of Tariff Reform), Sir Frederick Banbury was returned usuop- por.-ed, while at the. same time the Hon. Alfred Lyttelton, who was beaten at Leam- ington and Warwick, took his seat for St. George's, Hanover-square, vacated by tCol. the Hon 11. 'Legge. The principal measures brought. forward by the Government in the House of Commons were the Workmen's Com- pensation Bill, the Trade -Disputes Bill, and the Education Bill, a tremendous Conflict raging throughout the country over the latter.- Other Government measures were the Merchant shipping Bill, the Plural Vot- ing Bill, the Land Tenure Bill, and the Town Tenants (Ireland) Bill. A number of ladies connected with the "Women's Suffrage" movement obtained ad- miesion to the Ladies Gallery of the House, and ertated . a scene, boitting and waving a flag with • the inscription, "Votes for Women." They wene removed and for- bidden to enter the gallery again. On the opcning of the Autumn Session a demonstra- tion was made on the part of the Women Suffragists in the Outer Lobby of the House, and several arrests were made. The next eoy ten ladies were charged- with creating a disturbance, and refusing to find sureties for their good behaviour they were sent to prison for two months. Two of them- were soon re- -I,niscd on the grounds of ill-health, but the others rem:lli-Mu in • gaol until liberated by order of the Home- Secretary, they having been there for a month.. A number of peti- tions aga%st the return of members arose out of the. General Election. That against Mr. Fell at Yarmouth was, dismissed, a simi- lar fate befell the one at Maidstone against the 'return of Viscount Castlereagh, at _Wor- cester Mr. Willianmoil. the Unionist member, was unseated, and at Bodmin the Hon. T. C. Agar-Robartes, who won the seat for the Liberals, was also unseated, while at Atter- cliffe the judges decided, that the Unionist candidate had failed to substantiate his peti- tion.

-CALAMITIES.

Unfortunately, 1906 has been remarkabla for the large number of disasters which have occurred in various parts of the world, the list including volcanic eruptions, earthquakes, fires, tornadoes, gales and floods, colliery ex- plosions, wrecks at sea, train disasters, with numerous minor casualties. Early in Ja-nu- ary there were earthquakes in Nicaragua and Hungary, followed by a volcanic eruption in Samoa. In February an earthquake and tidal wave Yiaited- Colombia, while a tornado swept the Society Islands. March brought news of the terrible raining disaster in the , Courrieres Colliery, at Lens, in France, by which over twelve hundred lives were lest, and soon after there was an earthquake in Formosa, some 1,200 persons being killed aud 2,000 injured. A mining disaster in Virginia caused the loss of 100 lives, and a similar disaster in Japan resulted in 250 being killed: April was the black mouth of the year, for it was then that the news arrived of the erup- tion of Vesuvius, by which many • villages wen destroyed, the total number of fatali- ties being unkntwn, while the ashes caused the collapse of the roof of Naples market, a number of persons being killed and injured. Another earthquake in Formosa killed hun- dreds, and soan after occurred the destruc- tion of San Francisco by earthquake and fire, with an enormous loss of life, 25 square miles being devastated and 500,000 people rendered homeless. May saw the wreck of H.M.S. Mon- tagu on Lundy Island, and over 100 spec- tators killed or injured in the attempt on King Alfonso and his bride '-^while during • June two persons were killed and many in- jured in a tramcar accident at Swindon, a similar mishap at Highgate causing the deaths of three people and injury to nineteen. In July the American line express, on the South-Western Railway, Was wrecked at Salisbury with the loss of 28 lives, twelve persons being. injured, while in the Wreck of a motor-'bus on Handeross Hill, nine people were killed and 27 injured. Fire in August at the Milan Exhibition caused damage esti- mated at £150,000, and 350 lives were lost in . the wreck of the Italian emigrant ship Sirio off the coast of Spain. Then came the earth- . quake in Chili and Argentina, in which, fortunately, the loss of life was not so heavy as at first reported, though great damage was i done to property n some eighteen towns. Fifty passengers' were injured in a railway collision near Glasgow, and in September• twelve were killed and sixteen Injured by the wreck of the Scotch express at Grantham, while two mishaps in America were respon- sible for much loss of life. .

A typhoon in 'Hong Kong, in which many persons were drowned and heavy damage done to shipping, was followed by a similar disaster in the. Philippines, severe storms shortly after in many parts of the world doing millions of pounds worth of damage, and causing the deaths of many per- sons. By the colliery explosion at Wingate, in October, 25 men were killed and 200 en- tombed, and the sinking of the Russian steamer Variag by a torpedo resulted in 200 passengers being lost. Over 800 men of a Japanese fishing fleet were drowned in a storm, and an electric train going through a bridge on the Pennsylvania Railroad y.'ss yesponsible for the deaths of a great nunaber -of people. A tremendous storm which swept the Rivieta in November did a great amount of dainao, and gales and floods were re- ported from various parts of the country. Foiky lives were lost by a collision of steamers off the Pacific coast of America, and the colliding of the Kaiser Wilhelm der l:rosse and the Orinoco at Cherbourg caused seven deaths, while Several persons were in- jured. In Germany a roburite factory was blown up by explosion, with the result that many persons were killed and injured, and enormous damage done to the surrounding property. CRIMES AND CIVIL CASES.

During this year there has boen no civil NM which could b. described as a eauss aslebre in the Law Courts, though a tre- mendous amount of interest was taken in the inquiry into the mental condition of the Mar- quis Townshend, when some remarkable evi- 'deuce was given as .to marriage brokerage. The inquiry resulted in the jury finding that Lord Townshend was not able to tannage his

...opoi or teeing care Itintself, and subsequently General Sir Red- rers Buller, who is an uncle by marriage of ihe Marquis, was appointed to manage the ostate. In connection with the arrest of a Madame d'Angely in the West-end, a Royal Commission was appointed to inquire into the 'Lilies of the Metropolitan Police, and a large lumber of people have given evidence. unerous witnesses were examined by the Commission which sat to consider the War stores scandal, and as a result of the find- ing of the Commission drastic punishment was awarded to officers and non-commis- sioned officers found guilty. Early in the year a case of "ragging' occurred among some officers in the Scots Guards, and seve- ral of them received punishment. A court - martial for the loss of the Montagu found Capt. Adair and Navigat- ing Lieut. Dathan guilty, and they were severely reprimanded and dismissed the ship. A serious riot occurred among the litokers at Portsmouth, and the ringleaders were court- rnartialled, sentences of from five years -to forty-two days being passed, while Lieut. Collard, whose order it was alleged caused the trorble, was subsequently tried. A deci- sion by the Court, of Appeal that the West Riding of Yorkshire County Council were justified in deductieg a sum from the salaries of teachers in non-provided schools for giv- ing religious education, caused a sensation in the echtcationa4 world, and the case was taken by the Government to the House of Lords. No crime has stood out above the ordinary level, but much sensation was cruised by Mr. Harry Thaw, brother of. the Countess of Yarmouth, shooting Mr. titan- ford White, a well-known architect, in New York.

FOREIGN AND COLONIAL;

Although there was no outbreak of hostili- ties between any Powers, home affairs caused much bloodshed in Russia, conflicts took piece between people and Cossacks, cases of revolt occurred in the Army, while mutiny arose in the Navy. Riots occurred in St. Petersburg, outrages were committed by re- volutionists, and bomb-throwing was in- dulged in on several occasions, the meet seri- ous being that at the residence of M. Stoly- pin, when about 30 people were killed and 60 Injured, including the Premier's two child- ren. M. Stolypin was unhurt, but the assas- sins were killed. In April the elections for the Puma commenced, and in May the first session of the Parliament was opened by the Czar, but His Majesty dissolved the Duma in July by Imperial ukase. Several plots against the Czar were reported, but none of them was carried out. In France M. Fallieree succeeded M. Loubet as President of the Republic, and the Dreyfus case was re- viied, the result being that the con- viction of the Captain was quashed, and he was decorated with the Legion of Hononr. In Egypt trouble was caused by the Sul- tan placing his troops in Egyptian Territory and refusing to withdraw them, but after re-, ceiving a ten days' warning the demands con- tained in the ultimatum were complied with. nritish officers were attacked by natives, and Captain Bull killed, for which o Egyp- tians were sentenced to death aeriti to im- prisonment. Trouble arose in South Africa with a Natal chief, 'Baralsaata, and a tribe under Siganandi revolted. British forces consequently moved out against them, and later severe fighting occurred, a large num- ber of the rebels being killed, until news was received of the death of Banibaata, while a heavy surrender of rebels followed the . death of Siganandi. The Natal Government early in the year resigned owing to the Colonial Office postponing the execution of twelve natives, but the sentence was carried out a few days later. In November came news that a Boer named Ferreira had headed a raid into the Orange River Colony, but a few days afterwards he and his followers were captured by the Cape, Mounted Rifles. • Sensation was caused in America by Presi- dent Roosevelt drawing attention to the revolt- ing conditions which existed in the Chicago packing houses, and much amusement arose through the President sanctioning a new way of spelling. Tronl.!e arose in Cuba, and so serious did the c ilook become that the United States intervened and American sailors were landed, while Mr. Taft, the United States Secretary for War, left Wash- ington in the interests of peace. •Hostilitiea were suspended, and America presented aa ultimatum that unless peace were secured there would be a military occupation. Ulti- mately Mr. Taft proclaimed himself Gover- nor, and established a provisional govera.; nient.

The promoters of intercom- CHANNEL munication between TUNNEL Eegland and France by SCHEMES, means of a tunnel under the Straits of Dover are just now much more active than they have been for many years past. They have set to work with a new hopefulness, produced to a large extent, no doubt, by the entente cordiale. The feasibleness of a Channel Tunnel is a question that has now a much better chance of being discussed in a reason- able spirit than it has ever had before. The time that has elapsed since the last agitation on the subject, more than twenty years ago, cannot be considered as time lost. A Chanel Tunnel con- structed as it would then have been would already have become obsolete. Oa the other hand, the necessity for such a wails of Com- munication has grotvn less. It is possible to cross from Folkestone to Calais, or vice versa, in boats with scarcely any vibration, in five minutes under the hour, and in almost any weather. The ingrained French dread of sea-sickness must surely be greatly diminished in such circumstances. The great vocalist who crossed the Atlantic and refused to come hack again, spending the remainder of his life between the civilisation of the Eastern cities and that of the backwood settlements, can be understood, even if he does not command much sympathy ; but the person who, having urgent affairs on hand, is afraid of an hour's sea-passage in a comfortable boat approaches incomprehensibly. The Simplon Tunnel has shown us what can be done by engineering in these d -vs, and with what speed, even under the most difficulties. It has also made all previous schemes of tunnelling under the Channel look hopelessly absurd in face of what can now be accomplished. The man who nowadays proposed to construct a tunnel with air- shafts, like chimneys, at intervals, for the vast sea-faring traffic of the Channel to avoid or to be wrecked upon would not merely be laughed at --he would almost to a certainty be sent to a lunatic asylum. Not much more feasible was the project of building a tunnel instead of boring it—of laying a sort of prolonged archway, that is to Fay, along the bed of the Channel. That such schemes to be reasonable enough for discussion up to about fifty years ago, or that anybody should have dreamt of building a bridge across the Channel, appears now to be I almost incredible. To read about thorn is like turning over the leaves of an old jest-book, and quite as amusing. Whatever else we have done I during the last half-century, we have certainly made the most amazing progress in engineering. Even the science of medicine cannot congratulate itself on greater or more rapidly succeeding strides. So far as the' engineering part of the business is concerned, therefore, the Channel Tunnel diffi- culty may be regarded as solved. Nothing but the discovery that the geologists have made a great error could convert the task of construction from a comparatively simple one to a work of a very serious character. The soundness of the project is such, from the engineering point of view, that abundance of money would be found for carrying it out should it be able to win approval from Parliament. The giving or withholding of that approval should depend, of course, solely on military considerations. As Sir ALFRED TURNEIt,ft distinguished soldier who is in favour of the Tunnel, has remarked : "If it would bring us into a position at all analogous to that held by Continental countries whose frontiers are conter- minous with those of other great military Powers, which could make incursions across their frontiers immediately on a declaration of war, the Tunnel scheme must be unhesitatingly condemned by all sane persons." As to that we are all agreed ; and as the scheme is at present engaging the attention of the Committee of Imperial Defence it is to be assumed that these are questions that are being discussed. It will be strange if the answer given to them does not greatly vary from that which r was given twenty-two or twenty-three years ago.

This has been a good, old- AN OLD-FASHIONED fashioned English Christmas CHRISTMAS. —something of a rarity in these days when we usually have recourse to the works of Charles Dickens and imagine the rest. With a keen frost and a super- abundance of snow to clothe the earth in a dazzling white mantle, the fireside festivities gained in zest and almost everywhere was merry- making. NOTES AND NOTIONS.

Congratulations to the Warrington Male Voice Choir on their victory at Chester Eisteddfod ! Oar local musical talent has certainly come to the fore of late years and Warrington has every reason to be proud of its male voice choirs.

* * * A word of praise is undoubtedly due to the fire brigade for the valiant efforts which saved the sur- rounding property while the theatre was in extremis. They were so promptly on the scene that there was some fear they would save the theatre also ! * * *

A poser was once put toifr. W. S. Gilbert by a correspondent who objected to the use of " coyf al " in one of the Savoy operas. "How can anyone be full of coy ?" he was asked, " Really, I don't know," was the reply, "but having recently come across the word ' bashful ' I am trying to discover how anyone can be full of bash." * * *

Wall-street estimates that Mr. John D. Rockefeller's income during 1906 has been £22 16s. per minute, or £12,000,000 in the year. It is a crime, as well as a burden, to possess such wealth. There would be I nothing unreasonable in putting a limit on incomes which show a tendency towards reaching such bloated proportions. * * * 1 It was pleasing as it was unusual to see such a large attendance at the Musical Society's concert on Satur- day evening. The wisdom of arranging popular Iprices was fully evident and, of course, a grand work like "The Messiah " is always popular at Christmas time. We hope this highly successful concert may be taken as an augury for more generous support of the Society's work in the future. * * * " I should say that there are three motives which lead men to go the House. A prominent Tory ex-Minister," says Mr. George Harwood, M.P., in "Cassell's Saturday Journal,' " put the case to me thus : A third of the 670 members are in here because they have axes to grand a third because member- ship of the House of Commons gratifies their ambition, while another third, or rather barely a. third, come here to do what they conceive is their duty. This division is not a bad one."

trade had enabled workingpeople to lay by the wherewithal to prepare for this season of feasting and revelry, arid to see the shops thronged with eaier purchasers of the manly good things so alluringly displayed was very cheering. There are some to whow, Christmas, alas, means a keener realization of their poverty- stricken lot, but the spirit of kindliness and generosity which is ahr..aid at this time enabled most of them to participate in the good things. In the various punlic insti- tutions Christmas Ddy was, as it always is, a red letter day, seasonable fare being served in gaily decorated rooms. To those who were unfortunate enough to be in the Workhouse the day brought its pleasures, and few indeed must have been the places where the joyous Christmas spirit did not penetrate in somedegree.

It has often been noted THE THEATRE FIRE. that Christmas - time is usually made memorable by fires and sudden deaths, or disasters at sea. The season this year has been no excertion,for early on Christmas morning Warringtou wss startled by the clang of the fire-bell and the clatter of the heavy engines as they were rushed down to the Ruyal Court Tneatro in Rylands-street. Here the fire was found to have taken good hold and before long this long-familiar build- ing was completely gutted. It still presents a bold front to the world, but the roof, stage and dressing rooms have been totally destroyed, and what is required is a new theatre. It was almost as badly required before the fire, for the old Public Hall never took kindly to its new rile, anti the theatre was certainly one of the most uncom- fortable, draughty, and inadequate places of its kind we ever visited. We are afraid that the first feel- ing of an uncharitable public on hearing the news which heralded Christmas Day in Warrington was one of thankfulness. " Best thing that c-Juld have happened to it " was the funeral oration from more than one mouth when the fate of the theatre was learned. Nevertheless there was a genuine feeling of sympathy with the proprietors, and with the travelling company "Sunday," in the heavy. loss both had sustained. We hope that, ere long, we shall see rise from the thne-honoured ruins of the old building a new, clramodious and comfort- able theatre which will be a credit to a town like W arrington.

The date has been fixed for THE FoltnlC ,MINQ the elections in Russia, and RUSSIAN ELECTION, the news is the more satis- factory because the date is near. Russia may have passed through the worst of her experiences—one would like to think that she had—but a determirate factor may be the election of the new Duma. On the We of the Duffle, because it means the development of a constitutional conscience in the people, the hope of Russia is generally supposed to centre. The constitutional principle is, in some sort, again to be asserted. The elections are to take place on about January 25th and February 18th, for primaries and secondaries. By all accounts, matters have been much quieter in the empire in the last few months than they have for a long time past, Russia has had another chequered year in that which is now drawing to a close, and one can only hope that, after so tragic a day, in the evening there may be light. Assassinations have continued. We recall with sympathy that the present Premier, M. Stolypin, lost two children in the bomb outrage which in August killed thirty persons and injured fifty more. The infection of revolution this year spread to army and navy— Sebastopol, Poltava, and Kronstadt—and the year, we cannot forget, has been the year of pogroms. It has seen tors of thousands of Jews fleeieg from Warsaw in panic for their lives. We may hope with the New Year that Russia will start a cleaner page. Another dissolution of the Duma might be a terrible thing. As the Prime Minister told the delegates of the Inter Parliamentary Union at Westminster: " The Dame, is dead—long live the alma I "

The admirable enterprise of THE SLEEPING the German Government in SICKNESS, sending a medical expedit- tion headed by Professor Koch, the eminent scientist, to Africa to investi gate the cause of and endeavour to find a cure for the terrible sleeping sickness which has been devastating German East Africa and the adjacent country has been rewarded substantially. Dr. Koch has been able to send to the Imperial Department of Health at Berlin a report in which he announces the discovery of a treatment which at any rate, has been attended by most favour- able results. The Professor has been at work with a serum—which he calls atoxyl—since the I beginning of July. The injection of the serum,he discovered in that month, gave relief to the natives affected with the sickness within six hours. He continued his experiments and treatment, and so ranch was the relief appreciated that the natives have been flocking to his camp ever since in [ multitudes. Dr. Koch aud his assistants treated 800 in Ufa first week of November, and then had 12,000 more awaiting treatment. It was impos- sible to cope with all appeals for medical aid. Dr. Koch is keeping several hundred severe cases under observation for somo months to make sure that no relapse takes place after the atoxyl treatment. The great promise of this report is the more welcome in Eogland because Dr. Koch acknow- ledges that his success has been largely due to the investigations of two Englishmen, Drs. Gray and Grieg.

The Town Clerk has this week issued the Corpora- ! tion year book for 1907. It is, as usual, replete with I municipal and general local information and is pre- I faced with a portrait of the Mayor, Alderman ' Smethurst. This latter hardly comes up to the standard of previous years, and apparently His I Worship is unable to wear his most natural expression in front of the camera. • * * The c' Heathens" are evidently hard to please. When the Corporation instituted the charge of a half- penny for the distance covered by their tram service in the village ue heard wild murmurings and threats of a boycott. This week when the heavy -fall of snow disorganized the service and the cars ran only tol Stafford-road they grumbled because they could not pay the extra fare and have the full ride. Truly some people are never satisfied. ** * • What a God-s.a.iail the snowfall of Christmas night must have been to the unemployed! Manchester and Liverpool between them probably employed 4,000 men in getting rid of the snow and spent close on 0,000. This is something like a relief grant, but if it had been voluntarily given to the workless as a Christmas donation what would the ratepaprs and the Local Government Board have to say? The snow is to be thanked for driving hunger away from many a door. * * * The general opinion locally was that the 'Tramways and Highways Committees have not risen to the occasion in dealing with the snowfall. Only half hearted attempts were made at clearing the streets, and the state of the tramway track shows a distinct want of enterprise on the part of those in authority. Many a a pound must have been lost in fares missed oiving to the disorganised service, and the money might iust as well have been earned by the unemployed in clearing the lines.

* Good fortune does occasionally come to• those who -are sorely in need of it. A great Christmas lottery for a prize of S:40,000 has been won by the Evora Workhouse inmates. All the paupers, numbering 1,000, clubbed together, each paving 6d., and bought a whole ticket with the result that each has won 5.•40. The Workhouse is now deserted, all the former inmates having • left. Their departure was made the occasion of great rejoicing, a farewell banquet being given, at which everyone drank champagne.

* * The snowfall on Christmas night was a good omen for the unemployed, particularly of Manchester and Liverpool. They saw in it the certainty of a few shillings, and straightway turned out into the bitter weather to earn them while all the rest of the city was making merry. By midnight the wayfarer at that hour found the streets empty of all but these silent groups of sweepers, themselves coated thick with snow. As soon as snow begins to fall the unemployed make their way to the police yard, so as to be " on the job." Before the snow had been coming down an hour on Tuesday there was a crowd of men applying for work, and very soon some 500 of thein were plying the shovel and brush in the central parts of the city. The men work in gangs of twenty, a foreman to each gang; they earn 54. an hour, and they work in shifts of six hours. "Jolly 'ard work, try)," said an old sweeper in Albert-square tl a sympathiser, " and little enough for it. Well, it's better nor nowt, and it's no use, a-stoppin' at 'ome to reiice when there's

nowt to rejice on."

o co , Medical practitioners seem to be agreed (Writes a pharmaceutical chemist in the ' Manchester Guardian ") that the staple remedy for influenza is quinine. Taken in doses of one grain three times a day before the attack has become too severe, this drug seems to enable the victim to throw off the disease. It does not seem so very-long since quinine was worth nearly £1 an ounce, while to-day the same quantity can be bought for less than Is. The reasons for this great drop in price, and the story of quinine, are not without interest and even a little romance. The buying and selling of quinine are often attended by as much excitement as the buying and selling of gold shares. Quinine is a speculative article,ancl hundreds Of thousands of ounces sometimes change hands in the course of a day with a difference between buying and selling prices of 1-161. to id. of an ounce, Of late, however, speculators have fought shy of this i.' market, and a few weeks ago the record price of 71d. , Per ounce was reached—of course for very large ',. quantities. * * * _ ' As for "dissolving the House of Lords," says the : "Daily News," the thing cannot be done legally. - What is dissolved at a dissolation is Parliament —both ,(''' Houses, and the only " constitutional way of getting rid of the House of Lords" that we know of is passing a Bill of Abolition through all its stages in both Houses, and obtaining the Royal assent thereto—unless a long succession of Ministries embrace the policy of creating no new peers and waiting until all the exist- ing peerages lapse in default of heirs ,• which would take time. The ways of getting the House of Lords . to agree to its own reform or to abolition are two: . (1) Catching the members individually and placing i,•. them under the influence of a hypnotic suggestion to T the desired effect;. and (2) conferring peerages of the United Kingdom upon a number of reformers or abolishers siaciently large to create a majority in the House for the desired policy. This plan was very nearly put into practice in order to bring about the passage of the great Reform Bill, but the Lords gave in at the eleventh hour and the fifty-ninth minute.

WARRINGTON

Town Council. The Council Meeting fixed for Tuesday next has been postponed until Tuesday the Bth prox. Personal. - We are pleased to hear that Mr. Luke Talbot, Chief Constable, is progressing most favourably and is out of danger. Popular Penny Concerts. A crowded audience assembled in Bold-street, Wesleyan schoolroom on Saturday evening when an excellent programme was given at the weekly popular concert. A good display of animated pictures was thoroughly enjoyed. Songs were rendered in an able manner by Mrs. Nurse and Messrs. P. Smith and J. Willett. Master Nurse caused much amusement with the humorous song " I'm tired." UNDER THE CLOCK for Good BOOTS. Advt.— Accident at Market-Gate. Considerable excitement was occasioned at Market- gate on Sunday afternoon when two motor cars had p, narrow escape from a collision. One car had pro- ceeded up Bridge-steet and had turned into Sankey- street when another car came up Buttermarket-street and turned into Bridge-street. Anxious to avoid a collision, one of the drivers turned his machine round suddenly with the result that it crashed into the shop of Messrs. Boots, chemists, smashing the woodwork. - ...Portunately none of the occupants of the car were 1 . injured. . A Long Tramp. . On Monday, Edward Arthur Stanley, who is walk- ing round the world for the " San Francisco Examiner," reported himself at the Warrington Police Office. He had that day arrived from York and was on his way to Liverpool. • He has i four years allowed n which to walk 650,000 miles, and expects to finish his task four months inside that period, which ends on March 4th, 1908. From Liverpool Stanley goes to London, and from there will sail to New York. He then has a walk of 3,291 miles. So far be has had excellent health and is in first class condition.

Warrington Eisteddfod. This interesting annual event will take place on New Year's Day (Tuesday). In the afternoon the childrens' choir and the Church or Chapel choir con- tests, will be held whilst in the evening the Male Voice Choir and the individual competitions will be decided.

NEW YEAR'S GIFTS in large variety, see windows.—J. S. GARLAND, 51 to 55, Bridge at., Warrington.—Advt. General Buller's Visit. The visit of Sir Redvers Bnller, V.C., to Warrington to perform the ceremony of unveiling the memorial to fallen soldiers, has been definitely fixed for February 21st. As we stated last week, it was the desire of the committee to arrange the function for February 28th, as that is the anniversary of the relief of Ladysmith, but the date was inconvenient to Sir Redvers. As the selected date is a Thursday, it will doubtless be a most suitable one for the townspeople. •

_ NEW YEAR'S GIFTS in _ large variety,- see windows.—J. S. GARLAND, 51 to 55, Bridge st., Warrington.—Advt. Warrington Male Voice Choir's Success. The members of the Warrington Male Voice Choir are to be complimented upon a highly creditable success at Chester Christmas Eisteddfod. Competing in the choral contest for male voices they secured the first prize of 2,15 and a silver-mounted baton. Mr W. S. Nesbit was their conductor. The other corn- peting choirs were Liverpool St. Cecilia, and Chester United Brython. The test piece was "On the ramparts " (Saintis), and in making his award the Adjudicator (Mr.' Williams, of Carnarvon) referred to his own experience in training choirs for that particular piece, and said the Warrington choir had realized what he himself land endeavoured to obtain. There was a splendid blend of voices and the quality was good, especially in the tenors. The soloists (Hr. C Trin, tenor, and Mr. Spruce, bass) both sang exceedingly well. Funeral of the Late Mr. F. Lawton. Tae funeral of the late Mr. F. Lawton, Swan Hotel, Winwick, took place at the Warrington Cemetery on Saturday. Mr. Lawton, who was 29 years of age, was one of the clerks employed in the offices of the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Company, Ltd., and was highly popular. The circumstances of the case were peculiarly distressing as a house had been taken, the furniture bought and all arrangements made ' for Mr. Lawton's wedding to Miss Woods, of Cobden- street, Orford-lane, which should have taken place on Sunday. The principal mourners on Saturday were Mr. George Beard (step-father), Mr. F. Norman and Mr. F. Dooley (brothers-in-lftw), Mr. J. Lawton (cousin), Mr. Thomas Lawton (grandfather), Mr. A. Lawton', of New Brighton (cousin), Mr. F. Lowe (uncle), Mr. W. Woods, Mr. Arthur Jones, Mr. W. Payne, Mr. 11. Cosgrove, Mr. R. Jolley and Mr. F. Brach, the two latter representing the Winwick Social Club of which the deceased had been a member. The Bewsey office staff of Messrs. Pearson and Knowles attended in large large numbers, eight of them namely, Messrs F. Mclntyre, F. Fieldhouse, J. A. Brooks, J. Cot, A. Robertson, J. McKenzie, J. Pharaoh, and J. Potter, acting as bearers. Others from the works included Messrs. P. C. Price, Walter Wood, A. Smith, C. Skinner, V. Mather, James Pharoah, and R. G. Finney. The funeral service in the chapel and at the graveside was read by the Rev. James Davies. The wreaths included one from the Bewsey Office Staff. A letter full of sympathy to the family and kind refer- ences to the work of the deceased at the offices has been received from Mr. James Dodds.

Nicw YEAR'S Girrs in large variety', see windows.—J. S. GARLAND, 51 to 55, Bridge St., Warrington.—Advt. Local wedding. There was a pretty wedding at St. Mary's Church, Great Sinkey, on Thursday, the contracting parties being Miss Annie Sumrnerfteld, daughter of Mr. James Surnmerfield, 240, Liverpool-road, Sankey Bridges, and Mr. Henry Meadows, clerk of works, Prince Consort-road,South Kensington, London, and son of the late Mr. George Meadows, of Banbury. The bride is very well known in the district being a member of Sankey Church choir. The Rev. J. R.,Jones (vicar), officiated. The bride was becomingly dressed in biscuit cloth, with petunia hat, and carried a shower bouquet of chrysanthemums and lilies of the valley. The bridesmaids were Miss Kate Meadows (sister of the bridegroom) and Miss • Horsfield, of Fleet- wood, who wore blue cloth dresses with hats to match. The bride was also attended by two little girls. —Miss May Summerfield (sister of the bride) and Miss Green, of Wavertree whose dresses were of white velvet with muffs and hats to match. . Mr. Thomas Summerfield (brother of the bride) dis- charged the duties of best man. After the ceremony a reception was held at the Winmarleigh Cafe, a large number of guests attending. Later the newly married couple left for Southport where the honeymoon is being spent, The bride's travelling costume was of blue cloth, with sable hat. Many presents were received, including a family Bible from the Vicar of Sankey. LADIES' BEST GLACE BOOTS AND SHOES, UNDER THE CLOCK, BRIDGE-STREET.—Advt. Death of Mrs. George Hearne. The death took place at Grappenhall on Saturday of Marguerite Alice, wife of Mr. M. G. Hearne, of the Inland Revenue, after a short illnaSs of little over a week: An attack of acute bronchitis brought on heart failure and accelerated the end. The deceased lady daughter of the late Mr. Charles Sudlow) was held in the highest esteem by a larg3 circle of friends, and respect was universally shown. in the neighbour- hood of Grappenhall as the funeral cortege passed. Cut off in the flower of life, her loss is bitterly felt, more especially as -two little boys of very tender age a-e left. Much sympathy is felt for Mr. Hearne in his sad bereavement.— Amongst those present at the Cemetery to pay a last tribute of respect were Messrs. J. Sudlow, brother ; B. Shearer and T. Sudlow, uncles ; R. Lockerbie, J.D. Leigh, F. Walker, brothers-in-law ; J. Sudlow and W. H. Bellian, cousins ; C. E. B. Sutton, E. J. Sutton EL Sutton, nephews ; Dr. G. Braide, Messrs. J. Smith, F. Kerfoot, G. Howard, W. C. Litton and S.Aulttland ; R. Price and J. W. Turner representing the Warring- ton Catholic Dramatic Society, W. Kilduff and repre- sentatives from the Inland Revenue Department, as well as many lady friends of the deceased The last impressive prayers were said by the Rev. Father Marrs, of St. Mary's, *Latchford, and the Rev. J. P. Whittle, an old and dear friend of the deceased. Many beauti- ful wreaths were sent, including a large Celtic harp with cut strings from her husband, and also from the little ones ; from Godfrey and Mother (Dublin), Robert and Tissa, John and Polly,. Frank and May, Ted and Sarah, Uncle Basil and Aunt Mary, Nellie • and Joe, Willie and Agnes, the family of the late Peter Leigh, Mr. and Mrs. Pdrkhouse, Mr. and Mrs. C. F. Bead, Mr. J. Bate, a large floral tribute from the officials of the Inland Revenue, Mr. and Mrs. Litton, Mr. and Mrs. G. Howard, "Maggie," Mr. and Mrs. J. H. Crofts, Mr. and Mrs. W. H. Duckworth Mr. and Mrs. F. Kerfoot,Mr. E. J. Sutton, Mr. and Mrsi . J. Smith.

Death of Mr. R. Henry. Many Warringtonians will hear with regret of the death of Hr. Robert Henry which took place at Liver- pool on Christmas day. Mr. Henry was the second son of the late Mr. James Henry of Wildersixml, and served his time in accountancy at the office of Messrs. T. and J L. Tunstall, Warrington. He subsequently went to Altrinchatn, and then to Liverpool. Mr. Henry was very well-known in the town hiving lyee'n a member for many years at the Conservative Club. The funeral took place yesterday at St. Thomas Church Stockton Heath, at 2.39 p.m. in the presence , of a number of relatives and friends including repre- sentatives of Messrs. T. and J. L. Tunstall.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

VICTORIA-ROAD COUNCIL SCHOOLS.

Before breaking up for the holidays last Friday, the scholars attending these schools were treated to a Christmas entertainment including an exhibition of limelight views, &c. The first part of an attractive programme consisted of a set of beautiful pictures illustrating the " Story of the Year." These were described by Mr. W. Vernon. Then followed a series of miscellaneous slides of poems, Christmas stories and comics. Daring several intervals, patriotic choruses, school songs and hymns were renderad by the children. The entertainments were conducted by the head teachers, Miss A. M. Kilburn and Mr. J. F. Moulton, assisted by members of the staff. The lantern was manipulated by Mr. H. NV. Bowker. Rounds of applause showed how much the scholars enjoyed the treat, and that the efforts of the teachers were greatly apprecia- ted. The national anthem brought to a close a very enjoyable entertainment. DEATH OF MR. G. HORSEFIELD, RUNCORN. The death took place at 4 a.m. on Friday last week of Mr. George Horsefield, coal dealer and licensee of the Anchor Vaults, Halton-road, Runcorn. The deceased had been ailing for some time, and a little more than a fortnight ago went to Liverpool to consult a medical specialist. His ailment had then reached an acute stage, and when he took to his bed a few days later there was no hope of his recovery. He had lived in Runcorn practically the whole of his life. He com- menced business as a coal dealer and canal carrier twenty-eight years ago, and six years later became in addition a licensed victualler. He was well-known and highly esteemed for many good qualities. He had a genial temperament and did many good deeds in a quiet way. The funeol took place at Runcorn Cemetery on Monday afternoOn.

===== 0002601_19061229_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
WIDNES EX-MAYOR HONOURED.

On Thursday afternoon last week an interesting func- tion was witnessed in the Council Chamber at the SVidnes Town Hall, when Alderman Samuel Quinn was made the recipient of a handsome illuminated address and a cheque for £167, subscribed by the burgesses in recognition of his serviees to the town during his thrc 3 years' mayoralty. The address is in a fumed oak frame with moulded border and is surmounted by the borough arms, with photographs on either side of the Town He'! Public Li brary,Technical Schools,interior of the Council Chamber, the Gladstone Memorial Fountain in Victor's Park, and the Transporter Bridge. The text of the address is as follows :—" To Mr. Alderman S. Quinn, Mayor of the Borough of Widnes, 1903-4-5-6. In the name of and by the direction of a large number of our fellow townsmen, signified by resolution passed at a meeting held in the Council Chamber in the Town Hall, Widnes, on the 12th day of December, 1906 we have pleasure in offering you our hearty congratulations upon the successful completion of your three years Mayoralty of this borough and expressing to you our hearty appreciation of your untiring devotion to duty, both as chief magis,rate and head of the municipality and on the exceptional ability, knowledge, and experience you have brought to bear in the administration of the town's affairs, We also gratefully acknowledge the time and labour so liberally bestowed by you in promoting the welfare of the town and your willing assistance and support to all movements for the good of the community and in particular the absolute impartiality and fairness with which you have presided over the meetings of the Town Council and the invariable tact, judgment and conscientiousness of purpose you have shown in the performance of the various duties of your office. And we beg to tender to you our most grateful thanks and to express the hope that you may long continue to be honourably associated with the local government of the borough. Presented this 20th day of Docember 190 G. S. Owens (Mayor), John W. Waring, J.P., Fredk. Neil, .LP., Pierce Tlllon, Pau! Cauldwell.
