CHRISTMAS MORNING BLAZE.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE GUTTED. £lO,OOO Damage.

Christmas Day was heralded in Warrington by this most disastrous fire which has occurred in the town for many years. Shortly after three o'clock in the morning P.C. Sankey was passing the Royal Court Theatre when he saw smoke issuing from the building. He at once raised the alarm and a brother officer, P.C. Chrimes, proceeded to summon the fire brigade. With commendable promptitude the firemen, under the command of Superintendent Turner, turned out with a couple of steam engines. An investigation showed that the fire had broken out under the stage and at that end of the building was raging fiercely. The brigade at once set to work and devoted their attention to subduing the flames and to ensuring the safety of the premises immediately adjoining the theatre.. An abundant supply of water was obtainable, and with both engines working at full pressure the pro- gress of the ilones was apparently checked, and a conflagration of the magnitude which it ultimately attained was unthought or. After about half an hours work it was thought that the outbreak had been con- trolled, when to the surprise and consternation of all flames broke out at the north end of the building, and in a few minutes the whole of that side was alight. With two fires raging the firemen 'rate faced with a serious task, and one of great responsibility, as every moment the danger increased of otber premises becoming involved. The woodwork and upholstery fell an easy prey to the flames, and it was quickly realized that the building was doomed. The men became exposed to great risk as the roof over the stage was well alight, but they stuck manfully to their task, working with a determination and courage which .reflected infinite credit upon them. The end was, however, near at hand as with a great noise half of the roof collapsed. For a moment the flames were subdued but it was only for a moment as again they burst forth with even greater fierceness to com- plete the work of destruction. Rising to a great height they illuminated the district for miles around and pre- sented a magnificent but awe-inspiriug spectacle Then with a crash, the remainder of the roof gave way and, with the four walls alone standing, the building could be likened in a huge cauldron full of fire from which tongues of flame rose high into the air to the accom- paniment of thousands of sparks. To do anything further was impossible and the theatre was left to its fate while the efforts of the fire brigade were devoted to the saving of surrounding buildings, in which they were successful. The cause of the fire, is altogether unknown and whilst several improbable theories have been advanced it will possibly remain a mystery. The loss is esti- mated at over .€lO,OOO, and is partly covered by insurance. The chief sufferers are Messrs. Johnson and J. Potter, the lessees, with whom much sympathy was expressed. EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON:

ASSAULT ON A RAILWAY DETECTIVE.

Herbert Cook, of Makin-street, Newton-le-Willows, was fined £1 145., at St. Helens Police Conti, last Thurs- day for being drunk on St. Helens Station and assault- ing Railway Detective Bott.

THE LATE MRS. CLARKE.

The funeral took place last Thursday at New ton-in- Makerfleld Cemetery, of Mrs. Clarke, of Liverpool, an old resident of Newton-le-Willows. Mrs. Clarke was a relative of Mr. Clarke who was trainer of horses for the Duke of Leeds and the Marquis of Westminister, and brother to Mr. W. Clarke, who trained for Mr. Townley Parker and the late Lord Newton. The deceased came from Alford Hall, Cheshire, to Lancashire, and con tinned in this part of the county up to her removal to Liverpool, where she died in her 81st year. Mr. Mayor and relatives to a large number were present at the funeral, at which the Rev. James Ryder officiated, first at the Church and then at the Cemetlry. Many years have passed since the deceased lady associated herself with Newton-le-Willows, but her connection with the town has not been forgotten. At the funeral were the deceased's daughters, her son (Mr. T. Mayor), Mr. W. Jones (nephew), Mr. and Mrs. Swetenharn, Mrs. Brown, Mr. Hoar, Mrs. Hues, Mrs. Clarke and family, Lowton, &c.

A special musical service was held in gt. Paul's Church on Sunday evening, when a number of interest- ing old carols were rendered by the choir. There was a large congregation, and the service was much enjoyed. Miss Nellie Anderton was the soloist, and she gave a finished and artistic rendering of " U was despised." The sermon was preached by the Rev. R. B. De Wolf. At the conclusion of the service the organista (Jr. E. Wilkes) played the " Hallelujah chorus."

There was a large congregation at Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church on Sunday evening when the service was o f a choral character. Selections were given from" The Messiah," the choir singing with effect several of the better known choruses from Handel's oratorio. Mr. Richardson was heard to advantage in " Every Valley " whilst ISliss M. Hipkiss ably sang " Rejoice greatly." Mr. E. H. Pearce took the bass solos and created a very favonnhle impression. In the solo "The trumpet shall sound "the cornet obligato was splendidly played by Mr. W. Rutter. Mr. W. Pearce presided at the organ. Bold-street Wesleyan Church. Selections from " the Messiah "were given at Bold. street Wesleyan Church on Sunday evening, when there was a large congregation. The choir ably rendered the choruses 'And the glory," "For unto us a child is born" and the" Hallelujah chorus," whilst solos were given by Mrs. Booth, who sang " Rejoice Greatly" and " know that mv Redeemer liveth," Miss Mabel Henshall, who gave" 0 thou that tellest " with fine effect, and Messrs. W. Warburton and Plumpton. Mr. F. Hickman presided at the organ. During the evening a brief address was delivered by the Rev, S. Pitt.

The choir anniversary in connection with Latchford Wesleyan Church was held on Sunday, when special sermons were preached. The morning service was con- ducted by the Rev. Charles L. Stowe, and at the even- ing service the preacher was the Rev. John Gunnell. In the afternoon a cantata entitled "The Nativity of Christ " was rendered by the choir, Mr. R. H. Nleldrurn, of Lymm, presiding. Special anthems and solos were given by the choir at each service, the conductor being Mr. E. Oldberry, and the organist Miss Hartley. The eollections were in aid of the choir funds.

Garner and • the Secretary also took port in the meeting. Special Christmas hymns were rendered in every hearty manner. —The Secretary wished for all the brotherhood "A very happy Christmas."

On Wednesday morning an elderly woman named Reid, of Mornington-street, Birkenhead, was found suffocated in her apartments. A paraffin lamp, which had been left burning on the table, became overheated and burst, setting fire to the contents of the room'.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

[By " Ovalist.")

The Warrington men have not got much profit out of the holiday matches this season. After a heavy defeat at Leeds on Saturday they entertained Leigh and secured the full points in both Northern and South-Webt Lancashire Leagues after a great struggle. On Boxing Day they ought to have had Liverpool City as visitors, but six inches of snow proved an obstacle that the Club did not care to face, so the match was cancelled. The ground could have been cleared of course, but it was questionable whether the gate would have paid the expense. The Liverpudlians are not a great draw by any means and many people would doubtless have stayed at home. The Warring- ton Club often gets blamed for over-cautiousness, but it is this canniness in the past which has resulted in the Club's sound position to-day. •

The result of Saturday's match at Leeds created somewhat of a sensation when it was m tde known in Warrington. No one expected the locals to go down by 27 points to 3, and the reasons why they did so tae some finding. This is the worst defeat Warring- ton have sustained for a few years, and it was totally unlooked for. There was no getting away from the fact that Warrington were well-beaten, both forward and back, but there was nothing like such a disparity between the sides as the score would suggest. If Warrington had had another ten points and Leeds half-a-dozen less the play might be fairly represented by the figures. It was one of those matches where one side could do nnthing right. When Warrington were attacking it happened several times that Leeds completely changed the scene by clever play aided by a lot of luck. Three times were tries got by Leeds from the Warrington men's passing, and the blame for this lay largely at Jerkins' door. His chief fault was in throwing the Idl away when tackled. Somehow if he passed back a Leeds man got it, and a try usually followed. Then there were three more tries as the result of forward rushes which Warrington seemed powerless to stop. Altogether the Wartington line was crossed seven times —a record for the present , season, and one which I hope will stand some time.

While Leeds got all the points, it could fairly be said that Warrington showed the prettiest combina- tion. The "Yorkshire Evening Post " said that one' movement which took play from half way to the line and led to Warrington's only try was worthy of the Sonth Idrcans, and the praise was by no means an exaggeration. Brooks,Dickenson, and Isherwood ran, passed and repassed with a fkill which completely beat the Leeds defence and wasloudly cheered. Where Warrington suffered most was at half-back. Jordan -bad to take the place of Lees at the last minute and was nothing like so effective as he would have been at threequarter, while, as already said, Jenkins threw away more advantages than he gained. I was pleased to see Berry play a much better game. He not only defended well with good punting, but ran strongly several times and did much more than usual in the way of turning defence into attack. It was at centre where Leeds did most of the damage. Llewellyn and Thomas were faster than, the Warrington men and whenever they broke through their raced away to m tke a certain try for someone. You would have thought Warrington had no centres at all at times, but it was owing to the way the Leeds pair snapped up a stray Pass and got through like a flash. They were certainly at the top of their form and blessed with what the billiard player would call some useful flukes. Young, the ex-Cardiff full back, gave a sparkling display and fully upheld the reputation he has made in Yorkshire this season.

One of the finest games of the season was seen at Wilderspool on Christmas Day. Instead of proving fairly easy prey the Leigh men gave Warrington a rare fright and actually, held the lead until three- quarter time In fact if one of the halves had not made the fatal err 'r of passing on his own line they might have boon leading yet. But that incident belongs t a later chapter. Warrington started as if they meant to sweep the floor with Leigh but after ten minutes hot work they only got a penalty goal. Leigh replied with a try and so did Warrington. Then the visit ,rs got another and at half time were leading by six points to fire. Again, bad Warrington had the worst of the luck. They could beat Leigh from end to end but they could not Cross the line, and it must be admitted that the defence against them was perfect.

The resumption c.f hostilities was the signal for another mighty onslaught on the Laigh line. Time after time the defence tottered but did not falhafid when during a brief rush to the Warrington end Whittaker pat Leigh further ahead with a dropped goal you could almost hear the grass grow. It was now threequarter time and everything depended on staving power. Wirrington gave no quarter and some brilliant play was seen in the Leigh half. Twice Brooks dashed for the line after fine combiaation and each time he was bundled in touch at the corner flag. The crowd was dancing with excitement. Play proceeded right on the Leigh iine and the defenders got the ball. A saving punt was expected, but no! One of the Wives passed back, Lees darted round the scrum and knock- ing the ball down scored a try which was a triumph of watchfulness and skill. The scores were now equal, but when Preston carefully kicked a goal the roar could have been heard miles away. It was all over with Leigh now. Warrington proved their worth again and again and the citadel was captured once more by Dickenson who literally threw himself over from Isherwood's pass. As Preston also got a penalty goal the final score was 3 goals 3 tries to I goal 2 tries inWarrington's favour.

The game was full of exciting incidents and at one time it looked as if Leigh would just secure the verdict. Warrington never slackened their efforts and were in the Pnd fitly rewarded. There is n doubt the better side won. Dickenson again played a grand game, while Isherwood sheered more of his old form in the closing stages. Lander was weak at times. Berry and the halves loth put in some fine work, Lees in particular being most effective On the Leigh side little Whittaker played a great game at half-back and was well-backed up by S. Johnson, Bennett and Clarkson.

It will be seen above that the holidays have brought about several changes. Runcorn have at last been deposed from the leadership. and the defeat by Oldham on the Runcorn ground must have been a rery unpleasant Boxing Day knock. There was only a point between the sides at the close, bat Oldham had had the better of the game, and their defence in the closing stages was great. Jolley missed kicking a couple of goals which were fairly easy, whereas Fergusson on the other side was successful in two attempts, and this just made the difference. The The first four clubs now are Halifax, Leeds, Runcorn and Oldham and, rather curiously they all ha ye to

visit Warrington. Our own chance of getting a top place is not very rosy,though the New Year programme is not overwhelmingly heavy. The stiffest matches away are Oldham, Halifax and Runcorn, and the team will need some weak places remedying if there is to be much hope of success in these encounters.

Warrington should conclude the 1906 programme to-day with a visit to Bradford, and it would be a fitting finale if they recorded their first victory on Yorkshire soil. Bradford are in a lowly League position, but I fear they will give Warrington a rough passage. Still, "it is a long lane, &c.," and a Warrington win in Yorkshire is over-due.

The negotiations for the transfer of a well-known full back to Warrington have been concluded, and on Thursday evening J. H. Tilley, of Widnes was signed on. He played far his old Club this week and has been selected to play for Warrington on the wing in to-day's match at Bradford. The full side is as flows: Berry, full back; Tilley, Isherwood, Dickenson an'd Brookes, threcquarters ; Lees and Jenkins, half-bicks ; B )ardm tn, Shugars, Preston, Thomas, Heath and Harmer, forwards.

The " A " team should hare been at home to Widnes Rangers, but there is no probability of the snow clearing. Unless there is a severe frost the ground will be got in condition for the Runcorn match on New tear's Day.

MUSICAL SOCIETY'S CONCERT.

"THE MESSIAH."

Handers great oratorio " The Messiah " is so inseparably associated with the Christmas season that the choice of this work for performance at the War- rington Musical Society's concert on Saturday evening; was a happy one. The public cordially welcomed it and elacaved their appreciation bv assembling in such large numbers that the Parr Hall was well filled. No doubt the Popular prices wisely arranged for the accasion had a great deal to do with the success of the concert which must have been exceedingly gratify- ing to the society whose efforts too often do not meet with sufficient encouragement from their fellow- townepeople. Still more pleasing is it to record that the performance was a thoroughly adequate one and maintained the best traditions of the society. The delight of the audience was unmistakably demonstrated and we think it may justly be said that all who were privileged to hear this rendering of Handel's master- piece will treasure the happiest recollections of it. The magnificent choruses and the beautiful solos were listened to with equal interest and Warringtonians have probably never had a better opportunity of realizing to the full the grandeur of the oratorio. The soloists all took their parts well. Miss Gleeson-White possesses a soprano voice of great range, power, and flexibility and her singing was one of the most enjoy- able features of the evening. She was particularly effective in the exquisitely beautiful airs " Come unto Him " and "I know that my Redeemer liveth " which she gave with the deepest feeling and expression. Considerable interest centred in the first appearance as soloist at a Musical Society concert of Miss Nellie Anderton, of Warriagton, who is a contralto of great promise. It is due to her to say that the high opinion we had formed of her talents was justified and Alias Anderten received a very hearty greeting. Her initial effort " 0 Thou that tellest " was pleasing, but she rose to the occasion far more in the wonderfully appealing "He shall feed His flock." She was heard to even greater advantage iu " He was despised and rejected," her interpretation being most sympathetic, Naturally the audience was much pleased with this success of a local artiste, and accorded Miss Anderton a great ovation. Mr. Charles Saunders is a very acceptable tenor, possessing a pure toned voice which he uses with fine effect. Although on Saturday a slight huskiness was notice- able, Mr. Saunders did justice to the items entrusted to him, his soulful renderiuga of "Comfort ye " and " Every valley " being in the best possible taste. He also strikingly brought out the pathos of " Behold, sad see if there be any sorrow like unto His sorrow " and was particularly forceful in the strongly contrasting " Thou shalt break them with a rod of iron." A. rare musical treat was afforded by-Mr. Robert Radford who is one of the finest bass singers we have heard for some time. He had to interpret several of the most difficult airs in the oratorio but each was admirably given, his full, rich voice being splendidly controlled. In "But who may abide the thy of His earning ? " he gave the audience a foretaste of hi t& powers, but he excelled in " Wby do the nations" which was sung with such spirit as to rouse quite is storm of enthusiasm. Another note- worthy effort was "The trumpet shall sound," a feature of which uas the trumpet obligato faultlessly played by Mr. W. Rutter, a member of the society. Of the work of the chorus it is possible to speak in terms of unreserved praise. " The Messiah is rich in opportunities for a powerful, well-trained chorus I and of these full advantage was taken. The singing was marked with precision and that attention to detail and expression so 1 essential to a successful performance. A fine volume of sound was produced and the voices were well I Winced. Particularly effective Were the juous strains of " And the Glory of the Lord " and " For unto us a child is born," while the culminating point of the first part of the oratorio—the triumphal " Lift up your heads, oye gates "—was highly impressive. The crowning effcrrt, however, was the " Hallejuah Chorus " which is so inspiring in its grandeur, and it was evident that it produced a profound effect on the large audience. Hardly less striking was the massive chorus " Worthy is the Lamb " which brings the oratorio to a fitting close. Mr. F. 11. Crossley wielded , the brlton with his custommy skill and he is to be warmly complimented upon the success of the concert which was in no small measure due to his training. The orchestra under Mr. R. H. Pearson's leadership ably fulfilled their part and did good service, notably in the Overture and the Pastoral Symphony. Mr. W. I. Payton, Mus. Bac. discharged the duties of organist with his usual ability and good taste.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

A_ HEARTLESS FATHER.

James Henry Melia, blacksmith, last from Sheffield. pleaded guilty to absconding from Warrington and leaving his wife and six children chargeible to the Union.—Mr. B. Shearer, relieving officer, said that prisoner's wife and children had been in the Workhouse since July last, and they had cost the Guardians no less than £45. Prisoner was earning good wages at his trade in Sheffield. It was the first time he had been before the magistrates, as the Guardians had given him every opportunity of paying the money.—Prisoner said he said he walked to Scotland and tried to get work in Glasgow and Motherwell. He got a situation at the latter place, but owing to an accident he was out of work for 13 weeks. He afterwards made his way to Sheffield and was in a good situation there now. He was willing to take his wife and children back again and live happily together.—Melia was sent to gaol for one soiseth with hard labour. DISTURBANCR AT BINIC QUAY STATION. Patrick Regan, collier, flurtonwood, was charged at Warrington on Saturday with having been drunk and disorderly on Bank Quay Station on Friday night end also with having assaulted Railway-detective Coward.— Detective Coward stated that he saw prisoner causing a disturbing. Prisoner went direct to the hooking office and stood so that people could not pass him. Witness asked him to go away and make a way for the people, whereupon be struck witness in the face. A postman saw that witness was in difficulties and went for a policeman, and if P.C. Jones bad not come on the scene he would have been roughly handled by the prisoner and his mates.—The Bench inflicted a fine 10s.

PRACTICAL SHIRT.

Pictured in our illustration is an extremely smart and becoming little morning skirt. The original was carried out in plain thin whit. cashmere, but any thin woollen material would serve equally well for tilit purpose, and the colour chosen is, of course, purely a matter of indi- vidual taste. Down the front of the blouse ran a wide box-pleat edged ott either side with

material, further adorned

with large fancy buttons. Over each shoulder came a shaped strap i of the cashmere, terminat- ing at the bottom n a point, and ornamented' with buttons. Below this strap came a stitchei tuck,, and beyond it a shaped piece of the cash- mere, stitched at the edge and finished with a button in the corner. The sleeves, of the bishop type, were taken into deep cuffs, finished at the wrist with little stitched straps and buttons. Mips BEALE'S FORTUNE.

The announcement that Cheltenham College will benefit to the extent of about £55,000 under the will of Miss Beale reminds a writer in the British Weekly that the most famous woman teacher of our generation was thrifty in her per. sonal expenditure. Miss Beale had excellent taste in dress, and would never have appeared ix public in the extraordinary hats and costumes which are affected by some leaders of the educational world. For years before her death it was her custom to wear only black. She chose rich and soft materials, and employed a gooci dressmaker. Miss Beale disliked to see her pupils wearing brilliant and conspicuous colours. An old Cheltenham student tells that in her time at college one girl appeared in a startling costume of scarlet. Miss Beale found occasion to .ddress the pupils on the subject of becoming aress. "There is one of you," she remarked, " who is wearing a dress which almost blind. me." Miss Beale was always on the watch for any indication of untthual ability in her pupils. Sometimes a mother would send an envelope to the Principal, containing the little „poems and essays of her child. Miss Beale never tossed these into the waste-paper basket, or contented herself with a curt note of acknowledgment. She took an interest in the girl and fostered her talent. To the end of her life Miss Beale fougi joy in reading the Hterary productions of all Cheltenham student.. DAINTY COFFEE COAT.

A charmingly dainty but f•-., inexpensive aril simple little coffee coat is the subject of our sketch. Such a garment is quite ideal for the home dinner or evening meal, and as it is cap- able of achievement by, the most inexperienced of dressmakers, and can be made of the least costly materials, is distinctly an economy as well as a dainty addition to one fs wardrobe. ODDFELLOWS DEMONSTRATION.

The Value of Friendly Societies.

Members of the Warrington District of the Grand I United Order of Oddfellows were on Saturday favoured with a visit from their Grand Master,Bro.William Tout, and celebrated the occasion by holding a public meeting in the Winwick-street Co-operative Hall. Probably owing to the season and to counter-attractions, there was only a small attendance. Bro. Thomas Farrington D.D.G.M., presided, and was supported by the chief officers of the various Lodges of the town. Bro. Tot; who had a hearty welcome, said it was perfectly true tlot the Warrington District of the Order had not made satisfactory progress for a number of years. There might be many reasons to account for that with which he was not personally acquainted, but ' there was one outstanding feature with which ho was struck in regard to the economy of the District. He was most concerned with the smallness of the amount of interest which they received for the surplus capital invested. It might he the beskthey could get with the limited amount which they had to invest, and as the same thing applied to other Districts an investment association had been formed in connection with the order, through which they could secure much improved rates. From the very great importance which the ques- tion of ins estment had upon their funds he hoped that in the near future they would give itattention. It was not to be denied that to-day young men were taking a 11,013 active and intelligent interest in their welfare than they did in the past, and made very close enquiries before entering any Society, in order to assure them- that they would receive the benefits for which they pnici, and therefore if they would improve their financial condition they would stand a far better chance of securing new membees. It was not only to the advantage of the District to look well to their invest- ments, but they had a moral responsibility in the matter. (Hear, hear.) They had no right to ask young men to join them unless they were certain that they could in after years give the benefits which they promised. Speaking on the Friendly Society movement, Bro. Tout said that people had too insular a conception of the movement. The Grand Order had a membership of 8511,288, with accumulated funds of £1,303.463, whilst during the last five years they had improved their posi- tion to the extent of increasing their assets by 2s. in the R. In the whole of the country about one in every three of the adult section of the community was a, member of a friendly society, so that they could easily ir6e that the moverhent ramified every nook and corner of the country to the material welfare of the people, who otherwise would have had to pay through the medium of the rates for the relief of necessitous people, by the operations of the poor law. (Applause). GRORCE WILDE, Grand Secretary of the Order, during the course of a somewhat lengthy address, said the Grand Order of Oddfellows was the oldest friendly society with branches to be formed in the country. aere were other Societies which were older than the Grand Order, but these did not establish branches until a much later date. 'The first dispensation of which they had any record in connection with the Grand Order was dated 1798, and as after a lap:3 of 103 years they were still growing, they had reason to believe they were not quite played out. Bro. WiLpit ably reviewed the progress of the Grand Order and mentioned, as one of the later steps which had been taken, the establish- ment of an insurance department. Probably one of the reasons why the Warrington District had not made so much progress as was to be desired, was to be found in the existence of such clubs as tontine clubs. He believed 'those club, were such a great rage because the people liked to have a good Christmas draw, but he told them esndidly that it was impossible to pay money into clubs for certain benefits and to get those benefits if more than half of what they rld was returned to them at the end of the year.(Hear, ear). Friendly Societies had proved times innumerable that the largest contribution was not suffioient to enable them to meet their liabilities and that they could not pay their benefits sinless they had well invested capital. It was therefore impossible to expect that tontine clubs ges as Friendly Societies andcond y egt i rveet et rhne tsha emt re fatcli nes: ttao the members at Christmas time. He said emphatically that it could not be done. It might very possibly be said "but they are doing it." Very possibly they vere for a time but they would not continue. When the members were young sickness was Debt but as the ago increased the liability became heavier end in order that the benefits might continue the contribution would have to be very largely increased or else the older members would have to be cleared out. Friendly Societies were determined to show the mischief which such clubs Would certainly bring about in course of time. ([Tear hear.) The country bad for years been promised old-age pensions but whenever that pension came they as Friendly Societies would have to take action to see if the Government pensions could not be supplemented by grants from their funds. (Applause). During the evening an excellent musical programme was submitted. This included selections which were admirably given by the Lyric Prize Quartette Party, solos by Miss Eastens end Mr. P. J. George, and humorous selections by Mr. A. Grounds.

MY DEAR NIECES AND NEPHEWS,

By the time you read this I suppose you will be quite in the middle of the fun and frolic which makes up a festive Yule. I wish I could drop like Santa Claus in amongst you, and watch you at your games—Snap Dragon, Hunt the Slipper, Twilight, and the like. Parties here, and parties there, I dare say, leave you very little leisure time. Well, I hope you are all contriv- ing to get as much enjoyment as possible out of the holidays before you enter into the troubles and trials of another year, including those terrible sums, awful dictation, and other scholastic torments; never mind, my dear friends, you all have the consolation of knowing that they don't last for ever. I had a very joyful surprise when I opened my letters this morning. One gontaining a request fOr membership all the way from—where do you thinkl—why, from South Australia. It was very nice to find that the League is extending in size, geographically as well as numerically. Although I have not much room at my disposal I feel I must print our new niece's letter to show you how it all came about. So with best wishes I conclude this week.

Just as these columns are going to press, I have received a splendid parcel of warm clothing, etc., from four members of the League, Margaret Constance Knight, Helena D. Knight, James Ashton Knight, and Gladys Knight, and all of one family, and to whom I am sending a nice prize in consideration of their thoughtfulness. As this deserving cause closes by the end of the year, I shall be glad if any of my nieces and nephews who have not yet sent things, and who wish to do so, will take advantage of the short time now left at their disposal.

UNCLE NED.

Dear Uncle Ned,—l am writing to wish you "Happy Christmas and glad New Year." I enclose a card I have painted in water colours. My fourteenth birthday is next Friday, December 14th. I am sending you two pieces )f poetry I think are rather good. The shops ocgin to look nice everywhere now. I am look- ing forward to next week's " Corner " very much.

THE BOY THAT GROWS. The boy that by addition grows, And suffers no subtraction; Who multiplies the things he knows, And carries every fraction; Who well divides his precious time, The due proportions giving, To sure success aloft will climb, Interest compound receiving.

I must now close, with all good wishes for you and the League, and .Christmas joy to all. I remain, your ever loving nephew, . HERBERT KING.

Dear Uncle Ned,—l should like to be one of your nieces. I am nine years old, and ant seud- ing you a piece of poetry about mice—little animals which frighten us children sc. From your loving niece, HILDA WLLLETT.

THE MICE. The merry mice stay in their holes, And hide there all the day; But when the house is still at night, The rogues come out and play. Now here, now there, they trot 'about; In every hole they peep, To see what they can find to eat, While we are Ist asleep.

They climb upon the pantry shelf, • And taste of all they please; They drink the milk, they eat the cream, They nibble Mead and cheese But if they chance to hear the cat, Their feast will soon be done; Off, off, • they b<ro to hide their heads, As 'fast as they can run.

The class in natural history being asked the difference between a dog and a tree, the head boy answered: "A tree is covered with hark, while a dog seems to be lined with it."

Once a teacher asked a class: "What are the four seasons ?" •

One boy replied: "Pepper, salt, mustard, and vinegar.' ,

WELCOME TO

Ida Bow, Edward Gardner, Florence Stevens, Gwendoline Kivell, Frederick Brown, Nellie Atkin, Margaret Whitaker.

Dear Uncle Ned,—l have seen your "Chil- dren's Corner" in the newspaper, and have been very interested in it. I should be very pleased to become one of your members. From your loving niece,

Ins Bow.

P.S.—I am sending a piece of poetry and a story, hoping to see it in print.

HONESTY.

man of China once bought a piece of land for which he gave a large sum of money. When ploughing it he came upon a heap of gold and gems buried in the earth. There was no one near when he found it, and he might have kept it himself. He was an honest man, and though he had bought the field, and had paid a fair price for it, he had not bought this treasure. He collected it together and...took it to the man from whom he had bought the land. He said to the man, "I have found this treasure in the field I bought from you, take them, they are yours." "No,". said the other, "they are yours, you bought the field and all its con- tents." For a longtime they each tried to get the other to take it, but neither would give in. At last the king was summoned, and he found that one had a son, and the other a daughter. It was agreed upon that the two young people should be married, and they were to have the treasure. That year the field bore a rich har- vest, and the ears of corn were larger than had ever been seen before.

, Dear Uncle Ned,--I am sending you a piece of poetry, entitled:

,;,1 THREE LITTLE FAIRY BOYS.. Teenie and Tfilie and Toddle Were three little fairy 'boys, All their toys were useful things, And their useful thing's were toys. When they got up in the morning, They lay quite still in their beds; They always wore their hats on their feet, And wore their boots on their heads., Whenever the three were hungry,

They always had something-nYdrink ; Their fates were brown and curly, And their hair was white and Pink. When they wanted to see things well, They always ,turned out the light; And they never said "Good Morning," Till the middle of the night., And when they wished to be silent, They always started to talk; And when they wanted to sit quite still, • They went for a long, sharp walk. They called their mother "Daddy," 7 And called their father "Mum "; And when they wanted to make a noise,

They all remained quite dumb. When they Wanted to play at football, They had their supper at noon; Turned the north and south poles into goal posts G iud made a ball of tlie moos.

COMMERCIAL FAlLURES.—According to " Kemp's Mercantile Gazette," the total number of commercial failures recorded in England and Wales during the week ending Friday, December 21st, was 158, viz. :—New Bankruptcy Proceedings published in the London Gazette, ST ; Deeds of Arrangement registered, 71. The respective numbers in the corresponding week of last year were : Bankruptcies, 90 ; Deeds of Arrangement, 93—Total, • 183 ; being a decrease of 25. The total number of commercisl failures recorded during the 51 weeks of the present year is 8,229 ; the total number recorded in the corresponding 51 weeks of last year was 8,870, shown a decrease of 6-11.

Warrington Thrashed at Leeds.

Warrington's visit to Leeds on Siturclay proved most disastrous, for the Wirepullers suffetei the most crush- ing defeat they have had for some seasons, and one that was quite unexpected. Though such men as Fish, Hockenhall, Lees and Taylor were absent, the team was considered strong enough to give Leeds a hard fight. The home lot were, hovrever,in great form and favoured by some luck they beat Warrington by 27 points to 3. There was a crowd of about 4,000 and the teams were as follows :

Leeds—Young, back; Evans, Thomas, Llewellyn, and Rhodes, threequarter backs ;. Ward and Brayshaw, half backs ; Watts, Wainwright, ,Aunn, Birch, Webster, and Stead, ferwards. . -

Ward gton—Ber;y, back ; Lan do r, Isherwood, Dickinson, and Brooks, threequarter hacks ; Jordan and Jenkins, half backs ; Preston, Boardman,Thomas, Beath Naylor and Harmer, forwards. .

, geferee, Mr. W. McCutcheon, Oldham. Touch judges Messrs. J. M. Seclinan, Dewsbury, and H. Tomes, York. Leeds attacked immediately after the kick-off, and were unluckly not to score from a forward rush within the first two minutes as the bill was carried over the I line. A second similar rush, however, ended in Watts forcing his way over, Brayshaw failing at goal. A minute or two later Thomas broke away and.passed. I The ball was not taken, but Lunn was up and stftggled over, Brayshaw adding an easy goal. Then Brayshaw sent the ball over the bar from a penalty kick awarded against the visitors for palpable obstruction. The next score catne as the result of a curious bit of play. Clever passing by Leeds took the ball to the Warring- ton quarter, where Evans missed his pass. Landor tried to kick the ball away but missed and Evans picking up ran over easily. Smart punting by the Warrington backs and quick following up saw Young beaten at the other end. Harmer snapped up the ball and ran behind the posts but was ruled off-side. Just before half-time a forward rush euded in Webster scoring. The position was an easy one, hut Brayshaw sent the ball wide. At the interval Leeds led by 16 points to nil. Hedy in the second half Warrington were credited with their only points. A bit of fine passing and re-passing was seen between Isherwood, Dickinson, and Brooks, which resulted in the last-named going into touch. Then, however, the ball went right across to the other wing, Jenkins cleverly beating the home halves and sending on to Landor who galloped over with a try which was the result of a brilliant movement. Preston failed to add the goal. Immediately afterwards some clever passing between Ward,,Thornas, Llewellyn, and Rhodes ended in the last-named scoring. No goal resulted. Then Webster scored his second try after a fine forward rush. Brayshaw again failed with the goal hick. There was next a smart burst on the part of Brayshaw and L'ewellyn, the latter making a lot of ground, and then passing to Stead when facing Berry. The Leeds forward had no difficulty in scoring. Bray- shaw added a goal from a po4ition n.ar the poets. After this the play was even, and the defence of neither side was again penetrated. Thus Leeds won by 3 goals and 7 tries (27 points) to a try (3 points).

WIDNES v. BARROW.

Played at Widnes, before a very poor attendance. The opening exchanges were in favour of Widnes, and then the Barrow backs came away with a rush, and Fox was within an ace of scoring when he was collared. A com- bined movement on the part of the Widnes backs ended in Davies scoring a fry, but Tilley failed to improve. Widnes kept op the attack, and Barrow were penalised close in, but Davies felled to kick a goal from a very easy position. Half-time score :—Widnes 1 try (3 points), Barrow nil. The second half was all in favour of Widnes, but try as they. might they could not cross the Barrow line, owing mainly to the fine defence set up by Gifford. From a scrummage under the posts, however, Lally dropped a goal, and just before the finish Mellor, for Barrow, managed to kick a goal from a penalty. Result :—Widaes 1 goal 1 try (5 points), Barrow I goal (2 points). RUNCORN DEFEAT HULL. Played at Runcorn, before 1,000 spectators. In the opening stages the home forwards dribbled well into the Hull half, but Taylor and Wallace transferred play to the Runcorn end, where Butterworth relieved. After several scrummages in midfield Jolley' passed to Williams who rushed over and scored, Jolley failing at goal. Wallace and Burchell gained ground for Hull, and after several exchange kicks, Wallace dropped a goal from almost half-way. Resuming, Pad bury and Moran took play to the Hull end, where W. Carroll was injured, and left the field, Hull being left with only twelve men. After a scramble near the Hull line Deakin scored at the corner, Jolley again failing with the goal kick. Jolley, Wallace and Moran gave a fine exhibition of passing, but Wallace, Parry and Harrison replied with a similar display. In returning the ball Taylor gave Moran a fine chance, and he, after a short run, passed to Butterworth, who scored, Jolley failed to improve, but was succesful a few minutes later after a fine try .by Deakin. Half-time score: Runcorn 1 goal 4 tries (14 points), Hull 1 goals (2 points). After the interval Runcorn asserted themselves, and in quick succession Padbury; Butterworth, and Deakin scored trieselolley being successful with two of the goal kicks. Taylor had plenty of work to do, but defended in brilliant fashion. Parry and Cooke made several good attempts to break through, but without success. Before the close Dixon got over, but Jolley failed with the goal kick. Result :—Runcorn 3 goals 8 tries (30 , points), Hull 1 goal (2 points). CHRISTMAS DAY MATCHES.

WARRINGTON v. LEIGH.

===== 0002601_19061229_art0162.txt | included=true | included =====
Fine Game at Wilderspool

The Christmas Day fare at ‘Varrington att:acted a crowd of over 6,000. Leigh were the visitors and brought a strong team, while Warrington were also fairly represented. The weather was perfect, with the ground just a little on the hard side, but suitable for a very fast game. Early on Warrington were aggressive, and after Isherwood bad been pulled up on the line, Leigh were penalised for obstruction, and Preston placed a goal. The visitors retaliated, and good work by Molyneux and S. Johnson led up to Butler scoring. The game continued of a ding-dong nature, and the Outcome of splendid work by Jenkins and Dickinson resulted in the former scoring. Leigh again pressed, and smart passing resulted in S. Johnson defeating candor and Berry, the visitors crossing over with a lead of six points to five. Turning round Warrington forced the pace in effec- tive fashion, and repeated efforts to cross by Brooks were baffled by sound defence. Whitter put Leigh further ahead with a dropped goal. Eventually Lees scored cleverly, and Preston placed his side ahead. Leigh were next penalised for obstruction, and Preston placed a goal. From flue work by Isherwood,Dickenson also scored. Result :—Warrington 3 goals 3 tries (15 points), Leigh 1 goal 2 tries (8 points).

"TN BIRDLAND."

London: T. Fisher, I.7nwin, Paternoster Square 2s. In Birdland with Fiold Glass aril Camera " by Oliver Pike, is a fascinating volume, both for its subject matter, its author, and the agreeably chatty style in which he writes. It speaks well for he book that it is in a second edition. The illustrations are from photo- graphs taken by the author, and they are really beauti- ful. By the way there is a curious misuse of the word "lay", on pp. 53 and 64,Sarely "lie" in the proper word; unless in the latter case the past tense is intended, whtch seems doubtful. Of the book itself we have nothing but praise. The author is justly " down " on the wilful pilferer of nests, and the unnecessary and wholesale destruction of bird life. His method we have long judged to be by far the best for a serious study of natural history. Careful patient, plodding observations of the habits and habitats of birds will be ultimately rewarded with a rich store of first hand-knowledge. We only wish Mr. Oliver Pike, whose book we have much enjoyed bad given us some of his impressions on the colour of birds, and whether he bad noticed any modification therein due, say to locality, food, or any other cause. We cordially com- mend the volume to every lover of Nature's feathered singers.

THE LEGISLATION OF 1906.

By R. C. LEHMANN, M.P.

Mr. Dirrell last year, in writing cli " The Legislation of 1905,7 had some characteristic things to say on the "at- tenuated, . starved - cat - like appearance" presented by the yearly Statutes of Mr.l Balfour's regime. The red volume in I which year by year the Acts are collected / and printed had indeed become less and I less worth the 3s. which it costs the- studious citiy,,n, anxious, so far as he is concerned, to disiipate the legal fiction I that we each of us know what the law is. To be quite fair, Mr. Balfour, by getting , on to the Statute Book a minimum of Statutes, was for once keeping his pledge. In July, 1904, he had sought to rally and reassure his perplexed and wearied fol- lowers in the House of Commons by pro-1 raising them that if they would loyally go on voting for him he would, neither that I year nor next, "unduly burden" them with "overmuch legislation." No one can ever accuse him of not having been as bad as his word. Last January, however, a peaceful revo- lution took place. The country, only too glad at last to have the chance, armed Sir Henry Campbell - Bannerman with an unequalled Parliamentary —or rather (and t the correction makes all the difference) 1 House of Commons majority. Mr. Ball four's bodyguard of Tory Members, • fearful above all things that they would I be compelled to lead the strenuous legisla- Nye life, were scattered to the four winds, and in their stead came a solid phalanx of reformers. The legislators whose motto was -" What can't be cured must be en- dured" were displaced by others whose guiding principle is "What can't be en- dured must be cured." And even the House of Lords has not been able to pre- vent the results of the change being writ large upon the Statute Book. What the size of the red volume for 1906 will be it is impossible as yet to say, for this bird's-eye survey is made before the session of 1906 is over. There 'was so much to be done after an almost unbroken Tory period of twenty years, there was so much eagerness amongst Ministers and Ministeiialists to get to work to catch up arrears and to remedy grievances, whether created or neglected under Tory rule, that the limits of an ordinary session proved all too short. Parliament rose, as usual, in August, only to reassemble in October to give a couple of months' hard work to bills as yet not fully considered. One poor Tory Member has actually complained that it is "very hard on him," because "if this Parliament is distinguished for one thing more than another it is for its extraordinary attention to business."

FEBB.IIAIIY-A TIGI7ST.

. First let me deal with the legislation "., safely steered through both Houses before the August adjournment, reserving for the ,• second (though not secondary) place the ' more contentious body of legislation dealt -,-. with in the Autumn Sitting. ..: The Finance Act is the formal entry oil -, . the Statute Book of Mr. Asquith's first I Budget. In the nature of things the new • Liberal Chancellor of the Exchequer could .. not do anything very heroic. He was prac- tically bound to take over the Estimates of his predecessors, but he made a wise use of , his surplus of £3,000,000. The duty on tea , was reduced from 6d. to 3d.; the is. tax on ': exported coal was swept away alt6Sether ; from November Ist. Money was found for , the relief of certain districts where the education rate is, in sooth, an "intolerable ' strain," and for some excellent postal reforms. The Army Annual Act is ordinarily un- commonly like its predecessors, but this : year there was a new departure in that a . clause appears in it abolishing flogging in. - military prisons. The 'Justices of the Peace Act abolishes ▪ the property qualification previously re- quired of county magistrates. At last we ' • have got rid altogether of the notion that • the possession of property is any test of ,f.' the qualities of character, impartiality, . and responsibility which are required in a •'- magistrate. The broader the basis on which the magistracy rests, the freer it is ;. from tests, the more it will command and deserve respect. -, There are two measnres. of particular in- terest to agriculturists. The Dogs Act en- ables the stray dog to be seized and makes the dog's owner responsible for damage to it* horses and cattle, and for sheep-worrying. .• The dog is not entirely deprived of the : "free first bite" which up to now has 7 been his (and his owner's) privilege, but ' for the future the bite, if it is to be free, ” must be performed on a human being. In legal language, the farmer whose animals - are bitten no-longer has to prove that the • dog was known to possess a "previous - mischievous propensity." This Dogs Bill !' was first introduced in 1894, so slowly do 1 the wheels of legislation run with Toryism on the box. The Fertilisers and Feeding ;• Stuffs Act makes certain that the farmer is , , protected against fraud, since for the N. future he will know what it is he is buying. . The Labourers (Ireland) Act amends the , law relating to the provision of labourers' cottagers in Ireland by providing for more simple and efficient administration and '. cheaper loans. It was time that the Irish labourer got soMe attention from the State, which by the Land Act of 1903 had 't. done so much for the Irish landowner and . farmer. [ The Prevention of Corruption Act (intro- duced by Lord Halsbury, but adopted by the .... Government as a Government Bill) makes 1-- it punishable for an employee to accept .: bribes or secret commissions without his employer's knowledge, and the person giv- • ing the money or discount is equally !,. guilty. These secret payments -are/ the t curse of commerce, and it was high time -' that they should be checked by legislation. f. No one need be frightened into fearing that giving- or accepting the harmless Christmas box has been made punishable: .., The Revenue Act is important because . puts the very numerous manufactures - Which depend on the use of industrial. liot . alcohol on an equal footing with similar t industries abroad as regards the cost of the spirits. The keenest temperance re- former can look with equanimity on this " effort to increase the consumption of alcohol. This by no means exhausts the long list ••. of Government measures passed by August, but spttee forbids the mention of any more. Of the half-dozen measures introduced by f private Members, mention should be made Pz. of the Musical Copyright Act, which ;.- makes it illegal and punishable to sell un- authorised copies of copyright musical r compositions. , ~, $, THE AUTUMN SITTING. t First of all let, me deal with, three '. measures passed at the Autumn Sitting, - hich are all of fivet - class importance to e workers. • The Trade Disputes Act is a measure over which there has been 'a prodigious amount of , what. may be called pedantic . . . . .

excitement. Its ohict is to give -back to • the workers the right of effective combina- tion ,o 1 which they have been most unex- pectedly deprived by rccent decisions of the Courts; and this. is done by anynding the 14w Pi ron*piraey, by Husking pclweful

-, -

===== 0002601_19061229_art0190.txt | included=true | included =====
The Examiner, Saturday, December 29, 1906

picketing legal, and by me saieguarcring ox trade union funds. The workers get back in Council. schools and to continue de- nominational schools in uncle - school rights which they exercised for over a q u districts. The other measure rejected by the Lords this autumn is the Plural Voting Bill. This was to make the rule in Parlia- mentary elections what it is already in County Council elections—that an elector , should vote once and once only. The Lords, knowing that the plural voter was a good friend of the Tory party, promptly came to his rescue and to that of the Tory party. , Here again the Lords cannot be allowed . _ _

As it. stands, the Act is what is called a c,„onsolidatin,g one— it does not amend the • previous Acts of 1897 and 1900, but repeals them, so that in , this Act we have now the whole of the law as to this subject. The effect of re-de- fining "workman " is to compensate for the first time 6,003,000 workers, including workers in workshops and on transport service, shop assistants, seamen, clerks, and , domestic servants. •No compensation was , payable by the Act of 1897 in cases of accident disablinr, the workmen for less , than a -fortnight. That, period is re- '7l:.!ed to a week, whilst if the accident taisables for a fortnight, or more compensa- Ton is payable from the date of the acci- dent. For 1 the • first time provision is made for compensation for certain diseases con- tracted in certain dangerous employments. ! This is only the briefest outline of the outstanding features of a measure of the very greatest importance to the workers. No time could be found in 1905 to pass the very much less comprehensive bill in- troduced by Mr. Balfour's Home Secretary. gricultu, _ tioldings ' intro- duced as the Land Tenure Bill by a Liberal County Member, was a good deal altered in order to get it through the two Houses. It was bitterly opposed, since it was supposed to attack " proputty, pro- putty, prop." As it now stands, it is a moderate but useful measure. It seeks to put the relations between landlord and farmer on a more businesslike footing. The farmer is protected against damage from winged game, permitted freedom of crop- ping, and awarded a measure of compensa- tion in case of unreasonable eviction. Even in its modified (and as some think muti- lated) condition, it should help g:sod farmers without injuring good landlords. The Street Betting Act, introduced by a Liberal Peer (Lord Davey) in the Lords, is designed to get rid of street betting, a temptation that sorely besets thousands who are led to put into risk what they (and their wives and families) cannot afford to lose. legislation of 1906," but even so it would be incomplete and out of perspective if something were not added as to the two bills which have been wrecked by the House of Lords, that "mere branch of the Tory Caucus," as Mr. Chamberlain de- scribed it, only too truly, once upon a time "in his Radical days." When the Liberal Government came into office there was obviously one question upon which legislation was imperative. Mr. Jalfour had used the Khaki majority he obtained in 1000 "by Means of a trick" (as the lawyers say) in order two years later to place upon the Statute Book an Educa- tion Act which quartered the Voluntary schools upon the rates without either placing them under effective popular con- _rol or getting rid of sectarian- tests for teachers. The Liberal Ministry was armed by the electors with clear and full authority to put an end to this "in- tolerable strain" on the citizen, and the principal bill of the session accordingly was Mr. Birrell's Education Bill. Neces- sarily it was long and, complicated, but its main principles were easily intelli- gible. All public elementary schools sup- ported out of taxes and rates were to be under public management and control, all religious tests for teachers were to be abolished, the cost of denominational teaching was in no case to come out of public funds, and every parent was to have the opportunity of sending his child to a school of a public, not a denominational, character. The measure, though (as Mr. Birrell repeatedly said) it was an " unde- nominational " bill, was studiously fair to the denominational schools. None the less it excited the hostility of the Tory party. In the Commons it was read a second and third time by majorities of 200. The Lords, after reading it a second time, proceeded to " reconstitute " it (as the Bishop of Manchester said) in Com- mittee. For fifteen days the Lords per- iormed this process, with the result that it became a bill for which Mr. Birrell plainly warned the Lords the Liberal party had no use. There were rumours that the Lords would abate their demands on the report stage, but incited by Mr. Balfour, they only wrecked it all the more by proposing to allow sectarian teachint

Prominent Ironworker's Death.

The death occurred on Saturday of Mr. James Ashton, of The Poplars, Bewsey-road, a prominent ironworker. For the long period of 36 years he was employed as a roller at the works of the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Co., Limited, and was • very popular with his fellow employees, by whom his death will be regretted. He had been in a delicate state of health for some months. Much sympathy is felt for the widow and family in their sad bereavement The funeral took place at the Cemetery on Monday.

CHRISTMASTIDE.

THE FESTIVITIES AT WARRINGTON.

It has long been the custom to bemoan the dis- appearance of the old-fashioned Christmas. This year there has been no ground for complaint on that score, for Christmastide has been as old-fashioned as anyone could desire it. The prospects on Christmas Eve were not altogether encouraging for, following on a miserably wet day, there was a mugginess in the atmosphere which was not at all seasonable. Never- theless, the streets presented a scene of great animation. They were thronged with people, many intent on purchasing Christmes fare and presents, and the tradesmen reaped a rich harvest. The " waits "were also abroad, but judging from the comparative small- ness of their numbers, the good old custom of carolling—one of the great joys of the Christmas of hyegone days —is declining. When Christmas Day dawned a great change had come over the scene. During the night there had been a sharp frost, and there was an exhilarating touch in the air which made it pleasant to be out of doors. The usual festal services were held at the Anglican and Roman Catholic churches which had been tastefully decorated with choicn flowers and evergreens. Christmas Day is pre-eminently a home festival and there were happy gatherings round the festal board and merry games in which young and old indulged with zest. As trade has been good of late, there were few homes into which some evidences of this great season of j.)y did not penetrate. For those who through force of circumstances had to spend the day in the various public institutions, every provision Was made in order that they might al ways look back to Christmastide with pleasure. As the day wore on the colder it became, and in the evening snow began to fall. In a short time the ground was covered with a beautiful white mantle and when people ventured abroad on Boxing Day they found that the fall had been the heaviest experienced for some years, being many inches in depth. Some of the unemployed were set to work clearing the streets, but throughout the day the tram service was much disorganized. In the evening there was more snow and on Thursday, when the town resumed much of its usual work-a-day aspect, it was with consider- able difficulty that pedestrians made their way about. Altogether the Christmas of lffiffi was a typical old fashioned festival and the celebrations were every whit as hearty as of yore. HOT-POT DISTRIBUTION. _ _

_ • At noon there was the usual distribution of hot-pot, loaves and mince pies which had been generously pro- vided by the Mayor (Alderman Smethurst). It took place at the Drill Hall, where 500 people, representing over 100 families' were supplied, the hot-pot having been cooked by Mr. and Mrs. Pawls. Among those present were the Mayor and Mayoress, Mr. J. H. Smethurst and the Misses Smethurst, Mr. Firth (Police Court Missionary), Mr. Cox (N.S.P.C.C.). Mr. W. Hickman, Sergeant-Major Ingrain, Mrs. James Richardson, Mr. Bolton (wife of the doputy-mayor), Miss E. Bolton, Mrs. Forshaw, Mr. Fargher (inquiry officer),Mrs. Clay, Mrs. Watkin, (Sankey-street), Mr. T. Longdin, Mr. Herbert Longdin, and Mr. and Mrs. T. H. Spinks. Several of the above mentioned ladiesassisted in the distribution of the food. _ AT THE WORKHOUSE.

Christmas brings its joys even to the dwellers in the Workhouse and the festivities this year were most suc- cessful. The buildings had been very tasterully decor- ated, special attention having been devoted to the hospital which presented an exceedingly pretty picture. One of the rooms was a study in red—red mottoes ; red foot blankets, red artificial flowers, while a couple of the mottoes displayed showed the good feeling entertained towards the officials. They read " Con- tinued SUCCON to the master and matron (Mr. and Mrs. Awbery), and "A long and happy career to our doctor" (the resident medical officer). There was a very fine Christmas tree heavily laden with gifts for the children. The decoration of the Dining Hall was equally pleas- ing, streamers being arranged with beautiful effect. The principal mottoee were " Success to the Master,' " Success to the Matron," "No Cross, no Crown." The donors of evergreens, plants, toys, etc., wore :—Sir Gilbert Greenall, Mrs. Rylands, Mr. J. Charlton Parr, Colonel Buckton, Mr. F. Monks, Mrs. Parkinson. Mrs. Frost, Mr. George Mather, Mr. Powell (Fearnhead), Mrs, Clay, Mrs. Roberts, Miss Broadbent, Mr. Dutton, Mr. Matthew Seton, Mrs. Unsworth (3, Palmyra-square), Mr. J. Rymer Young, Mr. T. Jackson Lee, Messrs. Garnett, Mr. and Mrs. Smethurst (Briery Croft), Mr. Dutton (Sankey-street) ; and Mr. S. Wake- field, Midland Hotel, loz. tobacco for every man and woman _smoker.

Co Christmas morning services were conducted hy the Rev. R. B. DeWolf of Liverpool and the Rev. Father Ryan and were of an appropriately joyous character. -

At noon the Christmas dinner—consisting of good old English fare—was served, 431 inmates partaking. It is intorestng to note that no fewer than 4101 b of beef wel'e cooked, and there were also used 561 b of suet, and 5001 b of plum pudding. and 320 ounces of tobacco, and 611 ounces of snuff wore distributed. Among those present during the progress of the dinner were the Mayor and Mayoress, NE% J. 11. Smethurst, the Misses Smethurst, the Rev. W. and Mrs.' Bracecamp, the Rev. R. B. De Wolf, Father Ryan, Mr. W. Hickman (deputy Chairman of the Board), Mrs. W. J. Forshaw, the Resident Medical Oalcer, Miss Cromp- ton, the Master and Matron and members of the stall, the latter assisting to serve the dinner. After the repast several short speeches were delivered, the Rev. W. Bracecamp opening the proceedings with a short address in which he alluded to the p-esence of the Mayor and Mayoress—the mention of whose name wa3 greeted with cries of" God bless them," and cheers. The Mayon, who was recoived with cheers, paid a high compliment to the work of the Master and Matron and also gave some good advice to the inmates. It was, he said, a pleasure to the Mayoress and himself to visit the inatitation in an official capacity,and they hope to have the privilege of other visits. They all knew that that was Christmas Day, and they also knew who was born on that day—Christ who died to save all, rich and poor alike. - _ .

One of the male inmates proposed a comprehensive vote of thanks to the Mayor and Mayoress, the Rev.W. and Mrs. Braceeamp, the Master and Matron, and the other members of the staff. - -

This was seconded by a female inmate and carried with much heartiness.

The MASTER, in the course of his reply, said he was personally under an obligation to the Nlayor for his presence because it was the first time a Mayor of the borough had attended in his official capacity for many years. Although the inmates were incarcerated in that building they were not entirely dead to what was going on outside, and he was sure that officers and inmates would watch the development of his Worship's career with much interest. (Cheers.) He thanked the officers who had given so freely of their tune to make the day's proceedings a success. Personally, he had decided to devote the whole of the day to promote the enjoyment of all the inmates. (Applause.) The MAYOR replied on behalf of the Mayoress and himself, and interesting speeches were also delivered by the Rev. R. B. De Wolf, Father Ryan and the Resident Medical Officer (Dr. Flitcroft.) A smoking concert took place in the evening. T EIE INFIRMARY. Ever 3 thing possible was done to make the day a happy one for the inmates of this institution. The Beamont, Hatton and Hardy wards had been beautifully decorated for the occasion by the nurses under the superintend- ence of the Matron (Miss Watkins), flowers natural and artificial and evergreens having been displayed to the greatest advantage. At noon the usual dinner of roast beef and plum pudding was served and was thoroughly enjoyed. In the afternoon, tea was provided for the patients and their friends, and among others present were the Mayor and Mayoress, the Rev. W. and Mrs. Bracecamp, Dr. and Mrs. Richmond, Mr. and Mrs. Charles Smith, Mr. Frank Boscow, Mr. Victor Roberts, Miss Isherwood, Mrs. J. Lawton and several of the medical stall. Mr. Victor Roberts paid a visit to each ward in turn, and gave clever conjuring entertainments, and also kindly sang and recited. A prize of a box of chocolates was given for the best decorated ward, and it was awarded to the Hardy ward. The Mayor acted as adjudicator. Evergreens were sent by Mr. F. Monks, and presents for the children by Mrs. Crompton (Blackburn Arms), Alderman and Mrs. Forshaw, Mrs. Ferguson, Messrs. J. and W. Dutton, and others. PADGATE INDUSTRIAL SCHOOL. The festivities at this school were carried out wtih all the accustomed zest. The main room had been decorated with great taste and presented a most

===== 0002601_19061229_art0196.txt | included=true | included =====
inviting appearance. Holly, evergreens and cotton. wool had been utilized with much ingenuity, and with a number oi: skilfully designed mottoes the walls were, relieved of all their bareness. The pretty paper flowers with which the mottoes were worked were the handi- work of the children, who must have made some thousands during the course of the year. Among the good wishes displayed were' A prosperous Nev Year to our Matron ond Superintendent," and "Health and prosperity to our Guardians." The Church of England children who had been con- firmed attended the early celebration of Holy Communion at Padgate Church ; the Roman Catholics attended Martinscroft Church at ten, and the others Padgate Church at half-past. On returning the Oiristmas dinner of roast beef and plum pudding was served and the children attacked it with avidity. Some of the Guardians were present to assist in promptly attending to the wants of the chil- .dren, who, on taking their seats, each found a Christmas greeting sent by sympathetic friends. After dinner, oranges, nuts and sweets were distributed. In the afternoon the children enjoyed themselves in the open air, and at five o'clock wet() prepared for the good things provided at the tea table. The evening, commencing at six, was taken up with a most enjoyable 'concert. It was entirely supplied by the children, who had been admirably trained by Miss Ivey (school- mistress). There was a set of lancers, a Spanish dance. recitations, songs, a capitally rendered cantata entitled " The Christmas Fairy," and, to wind up, a very pretty song," The Christmas rose," was given. The accom- paniments were played by Mr.Wright,the superintendent. ORFORD BARRACKS, _

To " Tommy Atkins" Christmas is the season of seasons. Ever ready to enjoy himself, at this time of the year greatly increased facilities are offered for the search of pleasure and "Tommy " has little else to do but indulge in feastinr, and fun, and in this he excels. Many of the men at feasting Barracks were granted leave of absence to visit home and friends, but to those who remained the depleted ranks did not mean diminished enthusiasm. On the contrary everything was entered upon in the true festive spirit. The barrack room, as a rule, is not the most beautiful of places, but at Christmas time it underwent a complete transforma- tion. The bare walls were hidden under a covering of evergreens broken only here and there to admit of the introduction of seasonable mottoes and greetings. The ceiling was festooned with garlands of holly and brightly coloured paper whilst more or less unsightly gas brackets bad also to bear their burden of decoration. and when the ;nen sat down to a dinner of Christmas poultry and pudding it will be judged that an animated spectacle was presented. In the evening dances were held and merriment held sway until a late hour. CHRISTMAS ENTERTAINMENT. ANIMATED PICTURES AT THE PARR HALL An attractive entertainment has been presented at the Parr Hall this week, in the shape of, Messrs. Edgar Brook and Co's " Empire " Animated Pictures and variety programme. Those desirous of seeing the most up-to-date novelties in animated pictures are admirably catered for and the show has given great pleasure to the holiday audiences. The chief feature is a beautiful series of pictures illustrative of " Aladdin and • his Wonderful Lamp" in which the incidents of the old nursery tale are vividly depicted. The whole ends with a striking tableau. The humorous element is, of course, weil to the fore and the audiences have laughed heartily at the doings of Jones during his visit to the seaside and at the marvellous escapes of " The cat with nine lives." There are many other exceedingly good pictures, and " The French Steeplechase" may be singled out for special mention. Some splendidly illustrated songs are much appreciated. Prominent among the variety " turns " is Prof. Earl's clever ventriloquial entertain- ment, and another capital diversion is provided by the Orchids, musical encentriques. There will be a matinee to-day (Saturday) at 3 o'clock and the con- cluding exhibition will take place this evening.

AT THE POST OFFICE. The volume of work dealt with at the Warrington Post o.lice this year has so far exceeded all previous experiences that it would be quite impracticable to estimate the number of letters &c., which passed through the office during the Christmas season. As in former years, it was found necessary to engage extra premises, the Gymnasium being used entirely for parcel work from the 19th to the 26th lust, and the Conserva- tive Club Assembly rooms were also requisitioned. In spite of this, the head post office was found, as regards space, quite inadequate to deal with the enormous number of missives posted for the outgoing mail on Christmas Eve and also with the arrangements for tho disposal of the correspondence coining in for delivery on Christmas morning. The pressure began to be felt as early as the 19th inst. and kept increasing until Christmas, being particularly noticeable on Friday, the; 21st, when the local flrins were apparently clearing up for the week. On Christmas Eve the rush was at its height, for despite a considerable augmentation of the counter staff, the crowd never appreciably slackened, arid at 10 p.m., the hour for closing, seemed to be as great as at any part of the day. The work was, however, successfully and promptly disposed of. On Christmas Day a large nember of temporary post- men, who had received preliminary training, were engaged and horsed vehicles were also brought into service to deal with the extraordinarily heavy delivery. Notwithstanding all the extra forces employed and the numerous precautions taken to prevent delay, it was not found possible to deliver all letters—so great was their volume—even in some of the central districts, before noon. The whole of the correspondence was, I however, at its proper destinstion shortly afterwards. I We find that little advantage was taken of the advance I posting—a scheme devised not only for the relief of the department but in the direct interests of the public also, as it is manifest that if the Christmas posting con- tinues to increase in the rams ratio the time may come when it will be impossible to dispose of the whole of the correspondence promptly. Only 20,030 letters for delivery on Christmas Day were posted under the advance scheme, which does not represent a tithe of what remained to be dealt with later. STOCKTON HEATH BAND OF HOPE. On Wednesday afternoon the Junior Branch of St. Thomas's Band of Hope had a Christmas party in the schoolroom, kindly lent by the Vicar. Various games ware heartily joined in by the children until tea was ready when all sat down to an excellent tea presided over by the fon: lady workers—Mrs. Buckley, Mrs. Taylor, Miss Langley, M 135 Hallows and also by Mr. and Mrs. Williarus, after which the games were renewed and truly it was pleasant to hear the merry laughter and join in the innocent fun. During an interval of quiet, after refreshments had been handed round, bon- bons, kindly supplied by Mrs. Do.va, were given to each and soon with the assistance of the workers there was a cha age in the children's appearance with their paper caps,capes aprons,etc. At the close hearty votes of thanks on the motion of the Superintendent( Mr.Hallovvs) were passed to the workers and friends as mentioned and to Mr. and Mrs. A. Down who had not only supplied the bon-bons but provided each child on leaving with an apple, orange and mince pie so as to be able to share with their friends at home. This Society is trying to do a good work amongst the little folk in the village and while not asking for financial assistance, believ ia such Societies should,as far as possible be self-supporting, Mr. Hallows would be pleased to receive on behalf of the sick me nbers,ef whom there are always more or less out of about 150 children, any toys or picture books, .S.;c., the parishiouers have no further use for and so help to brighten the days of sorrow and sickness. WORKINGMEN'S MISSION, BANK QUAY. The senior scholars' annual treat was held on Wednesday evening in the schoolrooni. Over 300 sat down to tea, to which ample justice was done. After tea an adjournmeut was made to the chapel where a short meeting was held. Captain G. R. Crosneld occu- pied the chair. The Secretary's annual report was read, which showed that the school was in a prosperous con- dition. The number of scholars on the books was 760, number of teachers and officers 41, total 801, increase on the previous year of 78. The average morning attend- ance had been 241, increase on last year of 19 ; average afternoon attendance 532, increase on last year of 40. In the four top classs alone there was a membership of over 250. The attendance of junior scholars had been very good indeed, and over £lO would have to be expended during the next week or so for the purchase of book prizes for regular attendance during the year. The financial statement showed the income during the year as being .C3l 19s. 6W. ; expenditure, I £3l Its. 31. ; balatice in hand, £3 is. 3id. In addition I £lO had been subscribed for the support of a native I Mission worker at Miss Mary Reed's Leper Asylum, at Chandag Heights, India. The report stated that a series of Sunday evening lantern services had been held in the schoolroom, the services being chiefly for the poorer children of the district. The room had been crowded at each service, seven meetings already having been held. It had been decided to continue the lantern services each Sunday evening during the winter months.. On Christmas Eve a treat was given to about 450 poor children of the neighbourhood, when a bag, containing many good things, was given to each child who ettended.—Captain Crosfleld, in com- menting on the report, expressed the pleasure that it gave him to hear of the continued success of the work. He was heartily glad to hear of the special effort made on behalf of the poor children at the Sunday Evening Lantern Services, and he should take an early opportunity of attending one of the meetings. During the evening, Mr. F. Hickman rendered a few selections on the organ, and Mr. Millington, Mr. J. Willett, and Miss Oakley contributed solos and duets.—. After votes of thanks to the chairman and artistes, an adjournment was made to the schoolroom where games were indulged in, a very pheasant evening being spent. The junior scholars had their treat on Thursday evening, about 400 children sitting down to tea. After the room had been cleared, thereat of the evening was occupied by games, the little folks all going home with , the unanimous verdict that the evening had been a very happy one.
